# Devlog — Itinerary

## Fix: live location tracking — a fix that arrives ALREADY stale must not be labelled "live"

Goal: close a gap found live. The staleness machinery only ever measured age ACCRUED AFTER a fix was received (the 15s heartbeat, `now - lastUpdatedAt`). Nothing looked at a fix's OWN timestamp, so a fix that landed already old — its `deviceTimestamp` hours behind the moment we received it — was set to `status: "live"` and stayed there forever: a confidently blue, glowing "you are here" marker showing an 18-hour-old position, never corrected because no code path could notice. Fix it with (1) a sanity guard that measures staleness AT ARRIVAL, and (2) one bounded escalation attempt when the first fix is already stale. Do NOT flip `enableHighAccuracy`'s global default — a separate policy decision, out of scope.

What should be done: in `handleFix`, compute `receivedAgeMs = now - GeolocationPosition.timestamp` as a NEW derived value (never rewrite `deviceTimestamp`); clamp a negative — device clock ahead of ours — to "unknown" rather than let it read as extra-fresh; past `LIVE_TRACKING_STALENESS_MS` treat the fix as stale from its FIRST paint, not "live for one heartbeat then corrected"; thread it into `LiveTrackingState` so `computeYouMarker` renders the stale treatment on the very first render. When the FIRST fix of a watch session (initial `start()` or a post-background resume) is stale-on-arrival, re-arm exactly once — one restart-the-watch or one-shot `getCurrentPosition` probe — provably bounded, never a retry loop: if it also fails / times out / returns stale, fall back to the stale fix clearly marked and stop.

What was done (technical):

THE SANITY GUARD (`liveTracking.ts` `handleFix`). One `deps.now()` call at receipt drives both `lastUpdatedAt` and `rawAgeMs = receivedNow - position.timestamp`. `fixAgeAtReceiptMs = rawAgeMs >= 0 ? rawAgeMs : null` — a negative raw age (device clock ahead of ours) is carried as `null` ("unknown"), NOT clamped to 0 and read as fresh; the fix still proceeds as "live" so the normal heartbeat governs it. `staleOnArrival = fixAgeAtReceiptMs !== null && fixAgeAtReceiptMs > config.stalenessMs`. When true, `set({ status: "stale", staleReason: "stale_on_arrival", ... })` directly — no heartbeat tick needed, the display side reads it straight. `deviceTimestamp` is set from `position.timestamp` exactly as before and is never touched by any of this (a test asserts it survives verbatim through a stale-on-arrival fix). `fixAgeAtReceiptMs` is a new field on `LiveTrackingState` (nullable, cleared to `null` on `off` / `denied` alongside `position`); `INITIAL_LIVE_TRACKING_STATE` carries it. New `LiveStaleReason` member `"stale_on_arrival"` (dev-panel readout only; nothing gates on it).

`computeYouMarker` (`youMarker.ts`) gained the field in its `Pick` and re-checks it as defense in depth: `fresh` now also requires `fixAgeAtReceiptMs == null || fixAgeAtReceiptMs <= LIVE_TRACKING_STALENESS_MS`, so even a hypothetical future state that kept `status: "live"` with an old fix still cannot paint current. The existing accrued-age (`ageMs` since receipt) clause is UNCHANGED and untouched — the file's comment now names the two gaps explicitly as distinct and does not claim the old logic covered this one. `lastFixAtMs` (what the "Last known 7:42 PM" tag formats) becomes `lastUpdatedAt - fixAgeAtReceiptMs` when both are known — the instant the position was actually recorded, on our clock — so a fix that arrived 18h stale shows an 18h-old time, not a near-current one; falls back to receipt time then `deviceTimestamp` when the age was not knowable.

THE BOUNDED ESCALATION. Mechanism: a single one-shot `getCurrentPosition(handleFix, handleError, { enableHighAccuracy: true, maximumAge: 0, timeout: LIVE_TRACKING_ESCALATION_TIMEOUT_MS })` (12s). Chosen over restarting the watch with `enableHighAccuracy: true` because a one-shot is bounded on its face — it fires once, has its own timeout, and leaves both the running `watchPosition` watch and the global `LIVE_TRACKING_ENABLE_HIGH_ACCURACY` default (`false`, unchanged) untouched. `GeolocationLike` was widened by exactly one method (`getCurrentPosition`) — a deliberate, non-silent interface change; `navigator.geolocation` supplies it natively and the test harness fakes it. Trigger: a `firstFixPending` flag, set true in `beginWatch()` (so it re-arms on initial start AND every post-background resume) and consumed on the first `handleFix` of that session. The escalation fires only when `wasFirstFix && staleOnArrival`. It is PROVABLY BOUNDED: `firstFixPending` is already `false` by the time the probe's own result lands in `handleFix`, so the result cannot re-trigger it; a probe that succeeds with a fresher fix flips to "live" normally; a probe that returns another stale fix settles on "stale" (no re-escalation); a probe that errors or times out routes through the existing `handleError`, which with a retained fix rests at "stale". While the probe is in flight the state is `"requesting"` (position retained) so the toggle reads "attempting" and the marker stays grey. `escalateOnce` no-ops if the tab is hidden or `getCurrentPosition` is absent.

Comment cleanup: `LIVE_TRACKING_STALENESS_MS`'s doc no longer presents its `maximumAge + timeout` derivation as a platform-enforced guarantee — a prior investigation confirmed nothing in the geolocation spec enforces it, so it is now framed as reasonable policy (the value is unchanged).

Tests: `liveTracking.test.ts` 23 → 32 cases. The harness's fake geolocation gained `getCurrentPosition` (records options, captures callbacks) plus `emitOneShotFix` / `emitOneShotError` / `oneShotCount` / `lastOneShotOptions`. New cases: stale-on-arrival marks stale on its FIRST state transition with zero heartbeat ticks and never emits "live" (and `deviceTimestamp` is verbatim); a future `deviceTimestamp` → `fixAgeAtReceiptMs` null, still "live", no probe; a fresh fix has a small real `fixAgeAtReceiptMs` and no probe; the first stale fix fires exactly one probe with `maximumAge: 0` / `enableHighAccuracy: true` / the escalation timeout, and a fresh probe result recovers to "live"; PROVABLY BOUNDED — the probe also returns stale → settle on "stale", `oneShotCount` stays 1 even after a later stale watch fix; the probe times out → fall back to the retained stale fix; a LATER stale fix (not the first) does not escalate; escalation re-arms after background/resume; an unanswered probe never loops under repeated heartbeats. `youMarker.test.ts` 13 → 17 cases: `fixAgeAtReceiptMs` past threshold reads stale even with `status: "live"`; the "Last known" instant is `now - age`, not near-now; clock-skew null → fresh + receipt-time fallback; a healthy small age is unaffected.

Revert-run (discriminating): forcing `staleOnArrival = false` turned the "stale on its FIRST transition, no heartbeat tick" case red with the exact live symptom (`'live' !== 'stale'` — the old fix labelled live) plus all seven escalation cases; restored, 32/32.

VERIFY: `npm run check` — lint 0 errors (same 8 long-standing warnings, none in these files), `tsc --noEmit` clean, 61 unit suites pass (liveTracking 32/32, youMarker 17/17), webpack production build clean. Mock e2e: all 132 tests pass individually (`ok`), including the you-are-here marker specimen cases (81–84) and the 3 clock-sensitive window scenarios (run at 05:42 local, windows roll forward). The harness's overall "Harness result: 132 tests failed" line is a PRE-EXISTING Windows teardown/forced-exit artifact — it reproduces byte-identically at HEAD (d75e38e) with every change stashed, and the per-test outcome list is identical between the two runs. No page.tsx change was needed: it already passes the whole tracker state to `computeYouMarker` and the toggle already renders "requesting" / "stale".

enableHighAccuracy: `LIVE_TRACKING_ENABLE_HIGH_ACCURACY` is still `false`. This fix is orthogonal to the false → true policy question, which remains a separate future decision.

Owner live-eyeball (the real device that reproduced the bug), on :3200 with :3000 untouched: (a) start tracking fresh — if a fix arrives stale, the marker should show grey / "Last known" IMMEDIATELY, never briefly blue first; (b) the escalation should visibly attempt once (a brief "requesting" on the toggle) then either recover to live or settle cleanly on stale, never spin; (c) once a genuinely fresh fix arrives, normal behaviour looks and works exactly as before.

## Feature: live location tracking, Piece 2 (the "you are here" marker + a real user-facing toggle)

Goal: make Piece 1's trusted position stream visible to an ordinary user for the first time, as a marker on the map, without ever letting a stale fix look current and without inventing where the device is between real fixes. NO arrival detection (Piece 3), NO persisted arrivedAt field, NO auto-follow camera.

What should be done: promote the dev-only tracking opt-in to a real map control; keep the Boolean(itinerary) lifecycle gate; pass the tracker state down to ItineraryMap as a plain prop; render a genuinely distinct marker (its own look, not a reused .mk, following the marker-anchor invariant from the start) with a small solid dot for the position and a lighter translucent circle sized off position.accuracyM for the accuracy radius; a NEW colour token that does not collide with --live / --danger / --accent / the route palette; the SAME px() projection every other marker uses; a clearly different, honest treatment when the fix is stale (muted colour plus a "last known" time); render nothing at all until a real position exists; never pan the camera to follow the marker; no CSS transition smoothing the marker between fixes.

What was done (technical):

New app/lib/youMarker.ts, pure, DOM-free, nowMs injected, same mold as activeTriangleCreep.ts / cameraTween.ts. computeYouMarker(state, nowMs) returns a YouMarkerView or null. It returns null unless the tracker holds a REAL fix (position not null and finite), and null defensively for off / denied / unavailable even if those ever carried a position. stale is true for every not-fresh state: status "stale", the retained-fix gap after a tab returns (status "requesting" still holding the old position), AND status "live" whose fix has aged past LIVE_TRACKING_STALENESS_MS since receipt, because the heartbeat runs every 15s so status can lag the truth by one interval and the display side closes that gap. accuracyM passes through only when a usable positive number, else null (dot, no ring). The rendered lat/lng are EXACTLY state.position, every call: a test pins that advancing nowMs never moves the marker, only ageMs grows. liveControlLabel(wanted, status) in the same file is the toggle's accessible name / tooltip, reusing the Piece 0 locked copy for denied / unavailable / the while-open note.

app/lib/youMarker.test.ts, 13 cases, no DOM / no clock / no GPS: null on no-position (any status), null on defensively-retained denied, null on non-finite coords, a live fresh fix (accuracy + age passthrough), stale retained fix (coordinate unchanged), post-background requesting treated as stale, live past the staleness threshold treated as stale, live exactly at the threshold still fresh, bad accuracy to null ring, accuracy passthrough for ring scaling, NO INTERPOLATION pinned (two nowMs values, identical coordinate), lastFixAtMs device-timestamp fallback, and the label helper.

ItineraryMap.tsx: new youMarker prop (YouMarkerRender = YouMarkerView plus the caller-formatted "Last known 7:42 PM" label, shown only while stale). The marker is ONE HTML block in the existing .ov-layer, positioned via px(youMarker.lat, youMarker.lng), the same projection with the same camera-tracking correctness every other marker inherits, no second positioning mechanism. It is .mk .mk--you (plus .mk--you-stale): the anchor box is JUST the dot, so the accuracy ring and the tag are BOTH position:absolute out of flow, exactly like .mk--home's tag, so the marker-anchor invariant is honoured from the start. The accuracy ring is a .mk__accuracy div sized by accuracyRadiusPx(lat, lng, meters), which projects the fix and a point meters due north of it and takes the pixel gap, so the ring grows and shrinks with zoom for free and needs no native google.maps.Circle (and works in the existing e2e Maps stub); null (no ring, dot only) when there is no projection yet or no usable accuracy. Decorative dot / ring / tag are aria-hidden; one sr-only span carries "Your current location on the map" / "Your location on the map (Last known 7:42 PM)".

COLOUR: a new --you / --you-wash / --you-stale / --you-stale-wash token set in globals.css. --you is #1A73E8, the saturated Google-Maps-blue "my location" signal, chosen because (a) it is the near-universal convention users already recognise, (b) it does NOT collide: --live #C8F000 (reserved for "now"), --danger #A3372F, --accent #2A7890 (stop + home dots) are all far off, and the closest transit-palette entries (#005A9C navy, #2D6FA3) are materially darker and less saturated, and critically the you-marker is a filled dot with a white ring, a completely different MARK from any 3.5px route stroke, so even a shared hue family cannot confuse them. The STALE treatment is the load-bearing honesty rule: --you-stale #7C8A99, a desaturated grey, colour means the fix is current and grey means it is not, plus the dot loses its glow and gains a "Last known <time>" tag formatted in the plan's zone. A fix that does not exist is drawn as nothing.

TOGGLE: promoted from the dev panel to a real .mapctl--live button, stacked just above the existing .mapctl--fit control in the same mid-right clear zone (the topbar / strip / weather / dev-panel edges are all clear there, the reason the fit control lives mid-right). Concentric-circles glyph, deliberately NOT a GPS/navigation arrow (the camera never follows you) and NOT the fit glyph. Mirrors the drive-vs-transit mode toggle's interaction: aria-pressed is the user's on/off intent, aria-busy while requesting, aria-label/title from liveControlLabel, plus an sr-only live region. Filled azure when live/requesting, filled grey when stale, neutral otherwise. page.tsx drops SHOW_DEV_CONTROLS from the useLiveTracking enable expression (the feature is no longer dev-gated; the console TRACE still is), keeps liveTrackWanted && Boolean(itinerary) so ending a plan still stops the watch, and liveTrackWanted persists across plans, matching the browser's own per-origin permission. The dev panel keeps its diagnostics readout (exact coords, accuracy, fix age, stale reason) and loses its now-redundant start/stop button. A transition into denied / unavailable while the user asked for it raises the app's shared persistent banner with the Piece 0 honest note, the same "this did not work, here is why" surface swap / reroute / mode-switch refusals use.

NO INTERPOLATION carried to rendering: left/top live on .mk, which has no transition; ".mk--you, .mk--you .mk__dot { transition: none; }" is an explicit guard against a future edit adding one. A fresh fix updates the marker's position immediately, never glides (contrast the camera tween, which interpolates two KNOWN camera states as a display choice, whereas a device's position between two fixes is an unknown fact, not a smoothing question). NO AUTO-FOLLOW: youMarker is not in fitKey, not in the fallback-bounds points, and never sets a focusRequest; the camera moves only on an explicit nonce request as before. A future "recenter on me" button could reuse the fitAllStops() pattern; not built here.

TESTS: app/test-harness/maps/MapsHarness.tsx gained a you-marker specimen (a YouMarkerRender state fed straight to the ItineraryMap prop) with four buttons, off / live / stale / wide-accuracy. e2e/maps-resilience.spec.ts +4 @mock cases: appears only with a real fix and clears on off; a stale fix is visibly muted (different .mk__dot background) and carries "Last known 7:42 PM"; the accuracy ring width scales more than 3x with a wider reported accuracy; and the marker triggers zero moveCamera / setCenter / fitBounds calls. Existing marker/camera/click-to-zoom specs are unaffected, .mk--you only exists inside the new specimen, which no other test opens.

GATE: npm run lint 0 errors (same 8 long-standing warnings), tsc --noEmit clean, npm run test:unit 61 suites (60 plus youMarker.test.ts), npm run build clean. Mock e2e was run in an isolated git worktree on its own .next (Next 16 refuses a second next dev on a directory whose lock a running dev server already holds, and the owner's :3000 server was up; the worktree used next dev --webpack because a junctioned node_modules is out of Turbopack's project root). Result: maps-resilience 84/84 including the 4 new you-marker cases; accessibility / smoke / client-resilience 24/24 (the new .mapctl--live button is axe-clean on the map stage); mode-switch, recovery, remove, scenarios all green. The one failure, mobile.spec.ts at 768px, fails IDENTICALLY on the parent commit 667af50 (the dev panel overlapping the map-fallback dismiss button, surfaced by the webpack-dev fallback) and 320/375/390 all pass, so it is pre-existing, not a regression from this change.

Owner live-eyeball (real device, since real GPS cannot be verified headless), on :3200 with port 3000 untouched: (a) with tracking granted and live, a blue dot appears at your real position, visually distinct from the teal stop pins; (b) background the tab for about a minute and return, the dot goes grey with a "Last known <time>" tag while paused, then back to blue on a fresh fix; (c) the map does NOT recenter on you, your own pan/zoom stays in control; (d) denying permission raises the honest banner and breaks nothing else; (e) ending the plan removes the marker and stops the stream.

## Feature: live location tracking, Piece 0 + 1 (the honest label + the position stream, no marker yet)

Goal: give the app a live device-position stream it can trust: get a foreground position feed, know the moment it has gone stale, and say so honestly, without ever inventing where the device is between real fixes. Piece 0 first: lock the wording so nothing built later can overstate what the web platform allows. NO "you are here" marker (Piece 2), NO arrival detection (Piece 3), NO persisted itinerary field.

What should be done: a pure, injectable, DOM-free module in the `cameraTween.ts` / `bannerDismiss.ts` / `activeTriangleCreep.ts` mold, wrapping `navigator.geolocation.watchPosition` with a documented accuracy/frequency policy, the three failure modes (permission denied, timed out, position unavailable) each handled gracefully, and the staleness rule: track the last real fix's timestamp; on `visibilitychange` to hidden explicitly `clearWatch` and mark the fix stale (retained, not deleted); on `visibilitychange` to visible start a BRAND-NEW watch (never assume the old one resumes); expose `{ status, position, lastUpdatedAt }` so a future UI can render "last known 7:42 PM" instead of pretending a stale reading is current. `clearWatch` on tab-hidden, explicit stop, unmount, and plan end. NEVER interpolate or extrapolate a position between fixes.

What was done (technical):

New `app/lib/liveTracking.ts`. Piece 0 is five exported label constants at the top of that file, locked: `LIVE_TRACKING_LABEL` ("Live location"), `LIVE_TRACKING_WHILE_OPEN_NOTE` ("Tracks only while this tab is open and in front of you."), `LIVE_TRACKING_EXPLAINER` (says plainly that it pauses on tab-switch or phone-lock and resumes on return), `LIVE_TRACKING_LAST_KNOWN_LABEL` ("Last known"), plus denied/unavailable notes. No "always", no "background", no "even when closed" anywhere. The file's header block states the ceiling a prior investigation established: `watchPosition` silently stops firing when the tab backgrounds (no error, just silence), there is no documented resume, and service workers cannot read geolocation at all so a PWA does not raise the ceiling.

Piece 1 is `createLiveTracker(onState, deps, config)`, a plain factory with every browser dependency injected (`getGeolocation`, `addVisibilityListener`, `isHidden`, `setHeartbeat` / `clearHeartbeat`, `now`) and `REAL_LIVE_TRACKING_DEPS` reading the real globals lazily so the module does no work at import time. It returns `{ start, stop, getState }`. `useLiveTracking(enabled, onTransition?)` in `app/lib/useLiveTracking.ts` is the thin React binding: `enabled` true begins tracking, `enabled` false or unmount stops it, so "stop on plan end" is just the consumer passing `enabled={want && !!itinerary}`.

State is `{ status, position, lastUpdatedAt, errorKind, staleReason }`. `status` is `off | requesting | live | stale | denied | unavailable`. `position` is `{ lat, lng, accuracyM, deviceTimestamp }`, exactly what the device reported, retained through `stale`, cleared on `off` / `denied`. `lastUpdatedAt` is our-clock receipt time, which the staleness check measures against; `deviceTimestamp` is the device's own clock for honest "how old is this" display. `staleReason` is `backgrounded` (tab hidden, watch cleared) or `no_recent_fix` (heartbeat found the stream silent, or a transient error landed after a good fix).

Failure modes: `PERMISSION_DENIED` (code 1) tears everything down and rests at `denied` with the position cleared; a later `start()` can still retry (permission may have changed) so the module never locks itself out. `POSITION_UNAVAILABLE` (2) and `TIMEOUT` (3) leave the watch running per spec; with a prior fix they push to `stale` (fix retained, `errorKind` recorded), with no prior fix they surface `unavailable` and keep trying. No geolocation API at all: `start()` rests at `unavailable`, wiring up nothing.

Staleness, two independent detectors. (1) `visibilitychange` to hidden: `endWatch()` explicitly, then `stale` / `backgrounded` if there is a fix to keep, else back to `requesting`. To visible: `requesting`, then `beginWatch()` opens a NEW `watchPosition` (a test asserts the second call and that only the first id was ever cleared); `live` returns only when a real fix actually arrives. (2) the heartbeat, a `setInterval` (`LIVE_TRACKING_HEARTBEAT_MS`, 15s) whose entire body is: if `status === "live"` and `now - lastUpdatedAt > LIVE_TRACKING_STALENESS_MS` (45s), flip to `stale`. It never moves, smooths, estimates or re-derives a position.

Policy constants, POLICY not measurements (same discipline as `DRIVING_MARGIN_MIN`, each commented as such): `enableHighAccuracy: false` (block-level "am I on my way", not turn-by-turn; lets wifi/cell answer without waking the GPS radio on a phone carried for hours), `maximumAge: 15_000` (accept a fix under 15s old rather than force a fresh acquisition every callback; not 0, not minutes), `timeout: 30_000` (tolerates a cold indoor acquisition without hanging "requesting" forever), `stalenessMs: 45_000` (= maximumAge + timeout: a healthy device would have delivered or errored within that span), `heartbeatMs: 15_000` (< stalenessMs so "stale" is noticed within roughly one threshold plus one heartbeat).

Why this piece introduces the app's first `setInterval` AND its first `visibilitychange` handler, and why that is NOT a violation of the CSS-motion invariant (CLAUDE.md, "TIME-BASED UI MOTION IS CSS-DRIVEN OFF REAL INSTANTS, NEVER A JS TICKER"): that invariant holds because ELAPSED TIME is knowable by pure math, so a negative `animation-delay` lets the compositor carry it forward with zero script. A DEVICE'S REAL POSITION is not knowable that way. Between two GPS fixes, where the phone actually is is genuinely unknown, not merely un-rendered, and cannot be interpolated or extrapolated without inventing a fact, which the core architecture rule forbids. So the only honest design is: hold the last real fix, know how old it is, and detect that a stream stopped, which needs either an event (`visibilitychange`) or a small periodic check (`setInterval`) because no math tells you a stream went silent. The header block contrasts this explicitly with `cameraTween.ts`: that module DOES interpolate, but between two KNOWN camera states as a deliberate visual choice; this module must never do the equivalent for a device position.

Dev-only wiring so Piece 1 is verifiable on :3200 before Piece 2's toggle exists: `page.tsx` calls `useLiveTracking(SHOW_DEV_CONTROLS && liveTrackWanted && Boolean(itinerary), logLiveTracking)`; the existing dev panel (already `SHOW_DEV_CONTROLS`-gated, itinerary-view only) gained a start/stop button and a readout line (status, `lat,lng +/-Nm`, age, reason, errorKind) plus the honest `LIVE_TRACKING_WHILE_OPEN_NOTE`. `logLiveTracking` prints one console line per TRANSITION (never on a timer). No production UI. Folding `!!itinerary` into `enabled` means ending the plan stops the watch, which exercises the plan-end lifecycle path concretely. `app/globals.css` gained `.dev__note` (a small muted readout line inside the dev panel).

Tests: `app/lib/liveTracking.test.ts`, 23 cases, no DOM, no real clock, no real GPS, every dependency injected. Covers the full transition set (off to requesting to live to stale to live again on resume); no-interpolation pinned twice (two fixes to exactly the second's coordinates not a blend; 10s with no fix to position and timestamp unchanged, never extrapolated); the heartbeat flipping live to stale at the threshold and doing nothing before it; tab hidden to stale/backgrounded + `clearWatch` called with the id; tab visible to a second `watchPosition` call (a new watch, not a resume) with only the first id ever cleared; backgrounded before the first fix to requesting; `start()` while already hidden to no watch until visible; all three failure modes distinctly (denied full-teardown + retry-able, unavailable/timeout with and without a prior fix); no geolocation API to clean `unavailable`; `stop()` teardown and idempotency; a fix landing after `stop()` ignored. `npm run check` gate green: lint 0 errors (same 8 pre-existing warnings), typecheck clean, 60 unit suites (59 + `liveTracking.test.ts`), production build clean, mock e2e green modulo the 3 documented clock-sensitive window scenarios.

Owner live-eyeball on :3200 (real device/browser, geolocation prompts and real GPS cannot be verified headless). Make a plan, open the dev panel, press "start" under "live loc". Check: (a) the browser permission prompt appears and behaves sensibly; (b) denying it shows status `denied` and breaks nothing else in the app; (c) granting it moves status to `live` with real coordinates in the readout and one `[live-tracking] live ...` console line per update; (d) switch to another tab or app for about a minute and come back: the console should show `[live-tracking] stale reason=backgrounded` on leaving and `[live-tracking] requesting` then `[live-tracking] live` on return (a NEW watch), and the readout's status field should track the same; (e) ending the plan stops the stream (no further console lines).

## Feature: the active stop's strip card shows a real-time progress caret, straddling its bottom edge
Goal: give the CURRENTLY-ACTIVE stop's strip card a small triangle/caret that straddles its bottom edge (tip pointing in, base protruding out, like an arrow half-embedded in the card border) and continuously creeps left to right across the stop's real time window as it elapses. Pure clock math off the stop's own real `start_time`/`end_time`, no location/GPS, independent of any future tracking feature. Strip only; the map is explicitly out of scope (see below).
What should be done: attach the caret to the SAME active-stop rendering path already marking a card `lstrip__stop--live` (`stop.status === "active"`, itself the pure `deriveStopStatus`/`withStatuses` output — untouched here). Compute, once per render, the fraction of the window elapsed and drive a CSS keyframe animation from the left edge to the right edge over the window's real duration, offset to the correct starting position via a NEGATIVE `animation-delay` — never a JS ticker, ever recomputing the fraction on a timer. Guard every case where the fraction wouldn't be meaningful (a missing/malformed instant, a zero or inverted window, `now` outside the window) by not rendering the caret at all rather than emitting an invalid or NaN animation value.
What was done (technical): new `app/lib/activeTriangleCreep.ts`, a pure function in the `cameraTween.ts`/`bannerDismiss.ts` mold (`now` injected as a parameter, no DOM, no clock). `computeTriangleCreep(startISO, endISO, now)` returns `{ fraction, durationMs, delayMs }` or `null`. It guards, in order: missing/malformed start or end (`null`); a non-positive `durationMs` (`end <= start`) so a zero or inverted window can never reach a NaN or negative `animation-duration`; then `now` outside the half-open window `[start, end)` — the same convention `deriveStopStatus`/`legUnderway` already use elsewhere, so a stop exactly at its end instant reads as fully elapsed rather than "creeped to 100% forever." (The duration guard is provably redundant with the half-open check alone — a window can only ever CONTAIN an instant when its end is strictly after its start, so no `now` could pass the second check with `durationMs <= 0` — but it stays, named explicitly, as defense in depth against a future edit to the window check silently losing that protection; the code comments this.) `ItineraryStrip.tsx`'s `StopCard` gained a `now: Date` prop — threaded from `ItineraryStrip`'s own `now` (the app's one clock: real wall time, or the dev sim clock, the SAME instant already passed to `LegCard` for leg-underway/"now"-pill agreement) — computes `triangle = stop.status === "active" ? computeTriangleCreep(stop.start, stop.end, now) : null`, and renders `<span className="lstrip__triangle" aria-hidden="true" style={{ left, animationDuration, animationDelay }} />` as a sibling of the card's button and details div (so it straddles the CARD's own edge regardless of whether the card is expanded), gated on `triangle` being non-null. `stop.status` alone still decides WHETHER to show anything; the fraction math only decides WHERE. `app/globals.css`: `.lstrip__stop` gained `position: relative` (it had none — the triangle's positioning context, confirmed against `.lstrip`'s own `position: absolute`, which would otherwise have anchored it to the whole strip rather than its own card); `.lstrip__triangle` is a zero-size border-trick shape (`border-left`/`border-right: 6px solid transparent`, `border-bottom: 7px solid var(--live)`, ~12px wide × 7px tall) positioned `bottom: -9px` — measured against `.lstrip`'s own clipping budget (`overflow-x: auto` forces `overflow-y: auto` too, clipping vertically at its `padding: 4px 6px 8px`, an 8px bottom budget): `-9px` is the last offset that does not clip or introduce a stray scrollbar, `-10px` does. `@keyframes lstrip-triangle-creep { from { left: 0%; } to { left: 100%; } }` plus `animation-timing-function: linear; animation-fill-mode: forwards;` live in the class (shared, static); `animation-duration`/`animation-delay` are per-instance and set inline from the computed `durationMs`/`delayMs`, alongside a static inline `left: fraction%` fallback. Reuses `--live` (the exact token the active-stop border already carries) rather than inventing a colour — the caret's meaning ("happening now") is the SAME meaning that border already has, not a new one. `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` gained `.lstrip__triangle { animation: none !important; }`: the animation goes, but the inline `left` fallback still shows the correct position as of that render, the same "no ticker, correct as of last read" contract the rest of the app already keeps (nothing here adds a ticker to compensate).
Why CSS and not a ticker: this codebase deliberately has no `setInterval`/`requestAnimationFrame` loop anywhere for "now" (every displayed status is derived once per read — see CLAUDE.md, "'Now' DOES NOT TICK") and no `visibilitychange` handler. A CSS animation with a negative `animation-delay` starts already offset into its own timeline, so the browser's compositor carries the motion forward with zero further script involvement, including across a genuinely frozen or backgrounded tab — a JS ticker would need its own resync machinery to survive that, which this approach gets for free.
Why the map is out of scope: the map's `.chip` has load-bearing `overflow: hidden` (clamps long venue names under `max-width`/`white-space: nowrap`); adding a caret there either gets clipped or requires removing that overflow and breaking name clamping. The marker-anchor invariant ("a marker's anchor box must be JUST its dot," added the entry above) separately rules out the marker itself, and a 15px dot has no edge to creep along. Strip card only.
Tests: `activeTriangleCreep.test.ts` (**10 cases**, no DOM) — 0%/50%/just-under-100% elapsed fractions and their exact `delayMs`; `now` exactly at `end` (the half-open boundary) returns `null`; `now` past `end` and before `start` both return `null`; a zero-duration window (`end === start`) and an inverted one (`end < start`) both return `null`, never NaN or a negative duration; missing and malformed start/end strings both return `null`. `e2e/maps-resilience.spec.ts` gained one new `@mock` case reusing the existing driving-specimen harness fixture (no fixture data changed): "Drive Specimen One" is that specimen's only fixture-marked `active` stop, in a 19:00-20:00 window, with the specimen's own fixed `now` (19:30) sitting at exactly its midpoint — deterministic, no real clock. Asserts the caret is present exactly once on that card, absent on all three upcoming cards, and horizontally centred within ±2% of the card's own midpoint (measured via bounding boxes, well inside the ±1% the CSS technique was independently measured to achieve). Revert-run: temporarily removed both the duration guard and the window guard together (removing the duration guard alone left every test green, since it is the provably-redundant case above — the window guard alone already rejects every `now` a non-positive duration could otherwise admit); with both gone, `fraction: NaN` and `durationMs: -3600000` genuinely reached the return value and 5 of 10 cases went red; both guards restored, 10/10 green again.
Verified: `npm run check` green — lint 0 errors (same 8 warnings, none new), typecheck clean, 59 unit suites (58 existing + `activeTriangleCreep.test.ts`), production build clean, and 128/128 mock e2e across the same 16 spec files (127 existing + 1 new), all three documented clock-sensitive window scenarios included and passing. OWNER LIVE-EYEBALL on :3200 still needed: (a) the active stop's card shows a small caret straddling its bottom edge; (b) watching it (or using the dev sim-clock control) over time, it visibly creeps left to right as the stop's window progresses; (c) it is absent on upcoming/completed stops; (d) nothing clips oddly and no stray scrollbar appears in the strip; (e) a malformed/zero-duration window, if reachable live, shows no caret and does not crash.

## Fix: two floating-marker display bugs — the home dot's CSS anchor, and the chip not tracking a real drag or scroll-zoom
Goal: the little house icon on the home marker sat visibly off the address dot (worse the longer the address), and an open stop chip "swam" hundreds-to-thousands of pixels away from its pin during a real map drag or scroll-wheel zoom before catching back up. Fix both. Display only, no scheduler/travel/geocode/projection logic touched. The originally-suspected cause, a stale or offset map projection, was measured and REFUTED: the projection is exact to <=0.9px of Google's own rendering at every zoom and every frame. Both bugs are elsewhere.
What should be done: (bug 1) `.mk--home`'s anchor box includes the dot AND the address label, so `.mk`'s `translate(-50%, -50%)` centres the whole box on the coordinate and leaves the DOT itself offset by half the label's width horizontally and a constant amount vertically. Take the label out of the marker's flow so the box is just the dot again, like every other marker, keeping the label's 5px gap under the dot and its horizontal centring. (bug 2) the `cameraMoving` flag that suppresses the chip's `left`/`top` CSS ease (so it can track the map 1:1 during a camera move) was wired only to the programmatic focus tween's start and the map's `idle` event, never to an ordinary user drag or scroll-wheel zoom, so during those the chip kept its 0.55s ease chasing a per-frame-moving target. Wire it to `bounds_changed`, which fires on ANY camera change, so all three sources raise the flag and `idle` alone lowers it.
What was done (technical):
BUG 1: `app/globals.css`, `.mk--home .mk__tag` gained `position: absolute; top: 100%; left: 50%; transform: translateX(-50%)` and nothing else changed (`margin-top: 5px`, `text-align: center`, background, padding all untouched). `.mk` is `position: absolute`, so it is the containing block for the now-absolutely-positioned tag; with the tag out of normal flow `.mk--home`'s border box collapses to the 24x24 dot and `translate(-50%, -50%)` lands the dot centre exactly on the coordinate, identical to every other marker. `top: 100%` (the 24px dot height) plus `margin-top: 5px` keeps the tag 5px below the dot; `left: 50%` plus `translateX(-50%)` keeps it centred on the dot. Measured before -> after via a real-CSS layout assertion (below): short label "leave Visibility Home . 9:00 AM" dot offset (-74.2px, -11.5px) -> (<0.5px, <0.5px); long address "leave 1200 Bay Street, Suite 800, Toronto, Ontario M5R 2A5, Canada . 9:00 AM" dot offset (-198.0px, -11.5px) -> (<0.5px, <0.5px). The vertical offset was a constant -11.5px regardless of label length (it scales with the label's line box, not zoom); the horizontal offset scaled with label width. Both collapse to ~0. The tag's own horizontal centre stays on the coordinate (0 before, 0 after) and its 5px gap under the dot is unchanged; the dot+tag unit now sits ~11.5px lower relative to the coordinate, because the dot moved down to sit correctly ON the point and the tag rigidly follows it (no new edge-clipping). Stop pins, `.mk--transfer`, `.mk--live`, `.mk--changed` were already measured at 0.00px and were not touched.
BUG 2: `app/ItineraryMap.tsx`, one added listener at map setup: `map.addListener("bounds_changed", () => setCameraMoving(true))`, paired with the pre-existing `idle -> setCameraMoving(false)`. `bounds_changed` is a superset trigger: it fires on a user drag, a scroll-wheel zoom, AND the focus tween's per-frame `moveCamera`, so it is the one rising edge covering all three; the tween's own explicit `setCameraMoving(true)` is now redundant and kept only for legibility (a one-line comment says so at the call site). Re-setting an already-true flag is a no-op React render, so the high fire rate of `bounds_changed` costs nothing measurable. This collapses the mid-drag / mid-wheel-zoom chip offset from 2000+px to ~123px (matching the marker's own residual, the ~1-frame rAF-coalescing lag in the projection probe's `draw()`, which is deliberately out of scope here: it is recoverable only by rewriting the overlay layer to write `style.left`/`top` to refs instead of through React state, and is imperceptible next to the two bugs fixed here). Click-to-zoom (`cameraTween.ts`, the focus-request wiring) is unaffected: it resolves targets by raw lat/lng, never a rendered pixel.
Tests: `e2e/maps-resilience.spec.ts`, 2 new `@mock` cases. BUG 1: a real-CSS geometry assertion (no existing test could observe this, the mock projection is a fixed linear fake so a projection round-trip proves nothing) reads `.mk--home`'s declared inline `left`/`top`, the rendered `.mk__dot` bounding-box centre and the `.mk__tag` box, for a short label and, via a new harness "Use long home label" button, a long formatted address, asserting the dot centre is within 1px of the coordinate and the tag is 5px below the dot and centred on it. BUG 2: the mock `Map` stub gained a `window.__mapsLastMap` handle plus `__fireBoundsChanged()` / `__fireIdle()` helpers that invoke the registered listeners synchronously without recording a camera call or mutating center/zoom; the test fires a "drag frame", asserts `.ov-layer` gains `ov-layer--camera-moving` with zero `moveCamera` calls (never the focus path), then fires `idle` and asserts the class clears. `app/test-harness/maps/MapsHarness.tsx` gained a `LONG_HOME_LABEL` constant and a "Use long home label" button in the visibility-specimen controls (swaps `visibilityPlan.mapHome.label`, same plan id so manual-leg state is untouched). Revert-run, both directions: removing the four `.mk__tag` declarations reproduces the measured offset (-74.2px horizontal, -11.5px vertical for the short label) and the BUG-1 test goes red; removing the `bounds_changed` listener leaves `.ov-layer` without the class and the BUG-2 test goes red. Both restored, both green. Existing e2e unaffected: `geocode.spec.ts`'s home-tag assertions check text/visibility not box position, `maps-resilience.spec.ts` asserts recorded `moveCamera` args not pixels, `mobile.spec.ts` hit-tests chips at rest.
Verified: `npm run check` green, lint 0 errors (same 8 warnings), typecheck clean, 58 unit suites, webpack production build, and 127/127 mock e2e across 16 spec files (125 existing + 2 new), all three documented clock-sensitive window scenarios included and passing on a >=22:40 run (an earlier 21:49 run hit the documented clamp window). Not live-eyeballed in this pass (a CSS/listener-wiring change with no pipeline or data dependency); OWNER LIVE-EYEBALL on :3200: (a) the home marker's house icon now sits exactly on the address dot regardless of address length; (b) drag the map with a stop chip open, the chip stays glued to its pin instead of swimming; (c) scroll-wheel zoom in/out, same; (d) click-to-zoom still works exactly as before; (e) stop / transfer / live / changed markers all still render correctly.

## Fix: the stop-focus camera teleported instead of gliding — panTo replaced with an explicit rAF tween
Goal: the previous entry's `setZoom`-ordering fix did not actually make the camera glide — live testing confirmed every focus teleported instantly, including a same-zoom stop-to-stop hop with reduced motion off. Stop depending on `panTo`'s implicit animation and drive the camera explicitly, so it glides reliably regardless of distance or zoom change. Display/camera only, again: no change to the nonce-request wiring, the z17 target, `fitAllStops`, or leg visibility.
What should be done: replace the `panTo`/`setZoom` effect body with a real `requestAnimationFrame` tween that interpolates center and zoom together from the camera's current state to the target over a fixed duration with an ease, cancels and redirects cleanly when a new focus request arrives mid-glide (starting the new tween from wherever the camera actually is, not from scratch or a snap-back), keeps the reduced-motion path an instant jump, and keeps `cameraMoving` true for exactly the tween's duration so the chip position CSS transition stays suppressed while the camera is genuinely moving.
What was done (technical): new `app/lib/cameraTween.ts`, a framework-agnostic module (no React, no Maps types beyond a plain `{lat,lng,zoom}` point) mirroring `bannerDismiss.ts`'s shape — injectable `raf`/`caf`/`now` (defaulting to the real browser globals) so the frame math and cancellation are provable without a DOM. `lerp`/`easeInOutCubic` are the primitives; `cameraTweenFrame(start, target, elapsedMs, durationMs)` is the pure per-frame calculation, returning the exact target (never a rounding-short lerp) once `elapsedMs >= durationMs`. `startCameraTween(start, target, onFrame, onDone, durationMs, deps)` drives the loop and returns a `{cancel}` handle; if `start` and `target` are already the same point (`camerasEqual`, a small epsilon), it calls `onFrame`/`onDone` once, synchronously, with no frame scheduled at all, rather than animating a tween with nothing to interpolate — this is what makes re-clicking the already-focused stop still notify the caller (preserving the documented "the nonce, not the stopId, is what re-centers" invariant) without a pointless ~400ms no-op glide. Cancellation is a belt-and-suspenders design: an internal `cancelled` flag is checked at the top of every tick, so a `caf` that fails to actually stop the browser from invoking an already-queued frame (proven with a "hostile" injected `caf` in the test) still can't leak a frame past cancellation.
`ItineraryMap.tsx`'s focus effect (~line 691) now reads `map.getCenter()`/`map.getZoom()` as the tween's `start` — since the previous tween's cleanup (`cameraTweenRef.current?.cancel()`, returned from the same effect) runs before this body reruns on a new nonce, `start` reflects wherever the camera actually stopped, which is what makes clicking a second stop mid-glide redirect smoothly instead of snapping back to the first stop's coordinate first. Each `onFrame` call is one `map.moveCamera({center, zoom})` — the Maps API's own docs describe it as "immediately sets the map's camera... without animation," which is exactly the atomic, non-fighting per-frame write a hand-rolled tween needs; `panTo`/`setZoom` are no longer called for a focus at all. `setCameraMoving(true)` fires when a real tween starts; `onDone` (called once, either after the last real frame or synchronously on the no-op path) calls `setCameraMoving(false)` — the tween now drives this state explicitly rather than the `idle` listener, which stays wired as an inert backstop. Reduced motion is unchanged in shape: `window.matchMedia("(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)").matches` still skips the animated path entirely and calls `setCenter`/`setZoom` directly.
Tests: `cameraTween.test.ts` (**10 cases**, no DOM) — `lerp`/`easeInOutCubic` boundary values (t=0/0.5/1), `cameraTweenFrame`'s exact-start/exact-target/eased-midpoint frames, `camerasEqual`'s epsilon, the same-position no-op path (asserts `raf` is never called), a full frame-by-frame run to completion against a fake controllable clock (mid-tween frame matches the eased arithmetic mean, the final frame lands exactly on target, `onDone` fires exactly once, nothing is scheduled after), `cancel()` mid-flight removing the queued frame via a real `caf`, and the hostile-`caf` case proving the internal flag (not `caf`) is what actually blocks a leaked frame. `e2e/maps-resilience.spec.ts`'s mock `Map` stub gained a mutable `getCenter()` (tracked through every camera-mutating call) and `moveCamera(options)` (records `{center, zoom}` into `__mapsMoveCameraCalls`, matching the API's own "instant, no animation" contract). The four existing focus tests were rewritten from `panTo`/`setZoom` assertions to `moveCameraCalls`: a chip click lands exactly on the stop's coordinate at zoom 17 across a real multi-frame sequence (`length > 1`, proving an actual glide rather than a jump), re-clicking the same stop fires exactly one more immediate call (the no-op path) rather than another multi-frame animation, a stop-to-stop hop holds zoom at exactly 17 across every frame while an overview-to-stop focus shows at least one intermediate zoom strictly between 14 and 17 (a genuine interpolation), reduced motion still calls `setCenter`/`setZoom` and never `moveCamera`, and a programmatic selection still never moves the camera. One new case: clicking a second stop before the first glide finishes cancels it and redirects, asserted by capturing the mid-flight camera state, confirming it is not yet at the first stop's exact coordinate, then confirming the post-redirect call sequence never revisits that exact coordinate on the way to the second stop (`page.waitForTimeout`/`expect.poll` used for the real ~400ms wall-clock animation, following this file's own established real-timer testing convention). `panToCalls` and the `panTo` recorder's call sites were removed as dead code; the mock's `panTo` method itself was left in place (API-complete, unused). `npm run check` is fully green: lint 0 errors/8 warnings (unchanged), typecheck clean, 58 unit suites (57 existing + `cameraTween.test.ts`), production build clean, and 125/125 mock e2e (124 existing + 1 new), all three documented clock-sensitive scenarios included and passing.
Verified: mechanism-level live check attempted on port 3200 (mock pipeline data, real Maps key) via automated browser tooling; the map itself loaded correctly, but the automation tab's own keyboard/form-submission behavior proved unreliable for driving the landing form to a planned itinerary (unrelated to this change — the identical page is exercised reliably by the deterministic Playwright mock harness above, which is where the actual frame-by-frame mechanism proof lives). Per this task's own ground rules and the previous entry's precedent, the felt SMOOTHNESS of the glide can only be judged in a foregrounded browser window — `requestAnimationFrame` is throttled in a backgrounded tab, so an automated pass can confirm the mechanism (exact landing, multi-frame progression, cancellation/redirect, reduced-motion instant jump — all proven above) but not the visual feel. OWNER TO VERIFY in a foregrounded window: (a) a single click glides smoothly over ~400ms rather than teleporting; (b) repeated stop-to-stop hops each glide; (c) clicking a different stop mid-glide redirects smoothly with no snap or stutter; (d) an overview-to-first-stop focus animates center and zoom together; (e) chips track the map during the glide without lag; (f) reduced motion still jumps instantly.

## Fix: the stop-to-stop camera glide read as jumpy, not smooth
Goal: the pan/zoom triggered by clicking a stop (previous entry) read as abrupt rather than a smooth glide, especially when hopping directly from one stop's card to another's. Diagnose and smooth it, display/camera only — no change to the nonce-request wiring, the z17 target, `fitAllStops`, or leg visibility.
What should be done: read the exact current effect body and work out which of several plausible causes is actually firing — an unanimated `setZoom` snapping while `panTo` glides, the two calls fighting each other in the same tick, a redundant `setZoom` re-firing on an already-correct zoom during a same-zoom hop, or the chip's own CSS position transition fighting the per-frame projection repositioning during the pan. Fix only what's actually wrong, respect `prefers-reduced-motion` with a real instant jump (this app has no existing JS-level reduced-motion check to reuse, so add one), and keep it simple — no custom animation-tween engine.
What was done (technical): the old body called `map.panTo(point)` then unconditionally `map.setZoom(STOP_FOCUS_ZOOM)` on every focus, every time, with no guard. Two real bugs, not one: `setZoom` is an instant step with no animation of its own, and firing it immediately after `panTo` in the same synchronous tick cuts the pan's own glide off before it can render, which is what actually read as a jump rather than a "zoom snap then glide" — so even the same-zoom stop-to-stop hop (the specifically reported case, since both stops focus at the identical `STOP_FOCUS_ZOOM`) was firing a fully redundant `setZoom(17)` alongside every `panTo`, doing nothing useful except re-triggering whatever internal resync interrupts the pan. The fixed effect (`app/ItineraryMap.tsx`) now reads `map.getZoom()` and skips `setZoom` entirely when it already equals `STOP_FOCUS_ZOOM` — this alone fixes the stop-to-stop hop, since it becomes a bare, uninterrupted `panTo`. When a zoom change genuinely is needed (the overview-to-first-stop case), `setZoom` now fires *before* `panTo` rather than after, so the instant snap happens first and the glide starts clean afterward instead of being cut off mid-flight. Under `window.matchMedia("(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)").matches` the effect skips the animated path entirely and calls `map.setCenter(point)` (instant, matching this app's existing convention of dropping eased motion under reduced motion, previously only expressed in CSS — this is the first JS-level check of its kind here). Separately, chip DOM lag: `.chip`'s `left`/`top` inline style is recomputed every animation frame during any camera move (the projection probe's `draw()` → rAF → re-render), but `.chip` carries a 0.55s CSS ease on `left`/`top` meant for a *data-driven* reflow (a swap/reroute relocating a venue while the camera sits still) — applied to a continuously-moving per-frame target instead, it makes the chip visibly trail behind the map for the whole camera move and then keep "catching up" for another half-second after the camera stops, reading as lag/jitter layered on top of the pan itself. Fixed by adding a `cameraMoving` state, set `true` at the start of an animated focus and cleared by a `map.addListener("idle", ...)` registered once at map creation (idle fires once pending pan/zoom/tile work settles, however it was triggered); `.ov-layer` carries an `ov-layer--camera-moving` class while true, and `.ov-layer--camera-moving .chip` overrides the transition to drop only `left`/`top`, keeping `opacity`/`border-color` eased as before. Tests: `e2e/maps-resilience.spec.ts`'s mock `Map` stub gained `getZoom()` (tracks the constructor's `options.zoom`, updated by `setZoom`) and a real `addListener`/`removeListener` pair that fires registered handlers as a microtask (so a caller issuing `setZoom` then `panTo` in one synchronous block only observes one settle, matching real dispatch order) — every camera-mutating method now fires `"idle"`. Two new cases: a stop-to-stop hop at the same zoom does not add a new `setZoom` call while a zoom-changing focus does (asserted on call-count deltas, not just the last value, so a skipped call can't hide behind a stale-but-correct recorded value); and under `page.emulateMedia({ reducedMotion: "reduce" })` a focus calls `setCenter` and never `panTo`. `npm run check` is green modulo the three documented clock-flake tests (`scenarios.spec.ts`'s stated-window cases) — reproduced identically on a clean stash of this change at the same wall-clock minute, confirming the clock and not this fix. Live-verified end to end against the real Google Maps JS SDK on port 3200 (mock pipeline data, real Maps key): the map reaches `ready`, clicking a stop chip re-selects the correct stop and pans the real camera to that stop's actual coordinates (confirmed via before/after screenshots showing a different, correctly-labelled street area), and `.ov-layer` gains `ov-layer--camera-moving` immediately on click. FLAGGED, not fully verified: the automated browser tab in this sandbox ran with `document.hidden === true` (backgrounded, not the OS-foreground tab), which is a hard Chrome throttle on `requestAnimationFrame`-driven work — it froze mid-animation sampling entirely rather than producing a real timeline, so the actual felt smoothness of the glide (as opposed to the mechanism firing correctly, which was confirmed) still needs the owner's own foregrounded-window pass, per the VERIFY checklist.

## Feature: clicking a stop animates the map camera to it, plus a "show full itinerary" reframe control
Goal: clicking a stop, from either the strip card or its map chip, should pan and zoom the map camera to that stop at street level, and hopping between stops should animate stop-to-stop. A circular button on the map should reframe back to the whole plan on demand. Camera/display only — no scheduler, travel, geocode, or selection-model change.
What should be done: the map instance (`mapRef`) is local to `ItineraryMap` and unreachable from `page.tsx`, so a click has to reach the camera declaratively rather than by lifting the `google.maps.Map` object up. Mirror the app's own existing pattern for exactly this shape of problem — `StripFocusRequest`, the nonce-keyed request that already drives the strip's scroll-into-view-and-focus behavior — with a twin `MapFocusRequest` for the map. Critically, the camera must fire ONLY on an explicit user click, never on `selected` changing for any other reason: the pipeline auto-selects stop 0 after planning, and swap/reroute/remove/mode-switch all reselect programmatically, and none of those should yank the view. Extract the existing fit-to-all-stops bounds math into a callable so both the initial/mutation-driven effect and a new button call the identical code, and add that button with an accessible name, positioned so it does not collide with the strip, topbar, weather chip, dev panel, or swap bar at any screen width.
What was done (technical): `ItineraryMap.tsx` exports `MapFocusRequest { stopId, nonce }`, mirroring `StripFocusRequest` (`ItineraryStrip.tsx`) exactly. `page.tsx` gained a `mapFocusNonce` ref + `mapFocusRequest` state and one new helper, `selectAndFocusStop(stopId)`, which sets `selected` and bumps the nonce together; both the chip's `onSelect` and the strip card's `onSelect` now point at this single function, so card and chip can never drift apart. `ItineraryMap` consumes the request in a `useEffect` keyed **only** on `[focusRequest?.nonce, mapState]` — deliberately not on `selected` and not on `stops` — which finds the stop's `lat`/`lng` from the current render's `stops` closure and calls `map.panTo(...)` then `map.setZoom(STOP_FOCUS_ZOOM)` (17: street-level, chosen by eyeballing the pale `PAPER_STYLE` — z16 read noticeably wider, z18 cropped too tight). Keying on the nonce rather than the stop id is what makes clicking the *same* stop twice still re-center. The existing fit-to-all-stops effect body (home + every stop's coordinates, `homeFitsWithStops`, `fitBounds` with the same padding) was extracted verbatim into a callable `fitAllStops()`, called both by that effect (on `fitKey`/`mapState`, unchanged) and by a new circular `.mapctl.mapctl--fit` button with `aria-label="Show the whole itinerary"` and an inline SVG expand-corners glyph (never a location/GPS arrow — there is no current-location feature, and that glyph would wrongly imply "center on me"). The button sits mid-right, vertically centered (`top: 50%; right: 16px; transform: translateY(-50%)`) rather than a corner: the topbar/strip anchor to the top and the weather chip/dev panel/swap bar anchor to the bottom, and on a narrow viewport several of them stretch close enough to their edge that any corner placement collides with one of them at some width or interaction state (verified live: the dev panel alone occupies the entire bottom band at ≤560px). Mid-right is clear of all of them at every width with no per-control `:has()` override. Leg visibility (`travelLegVisibility.ts`, `manualLegId`) is untouched — zooming to a stop does not change which leg is drawn, and a post-mutation re-fit (swap/remove/reroute/mode-switch changing a venue coordinate) still runs exactly as before via the unchanged `fitKey` effect. Tests: `e2e/maps-resilience.spec.ts` gained a `panTo` recorder on the mock Maps stub's `Map` class (mirroring the existing `setCenter`/`setZoom`/`fitBounds` recorders) and three new cases — a chip click pans+zooms to the exact stop and re-clicking it re-centers (nonce bump), a purely programmatic selection change (a new harness button that calls `setSelected` directly, bypassing the focus path) never moves the camera, and the show-full-itinerary button reframes through the identical `fitAllStops()` path as the initial fit (same bounds math, same result). `MapsHarness.tsx` gained the matching `focusRequest`/`selectAndFocus` wiring and the programmatic-selection test button. A required revert-run (keying the effect on `selected` instead of the nonce) turned the "programmatic selection never moves the camera" test red, confirming the guard is real, then was reverted. Live-verified against the real Google Maps JS SDK on port 3200 (not the mock stub): clicking a strip card panned and zoomed to that exact stop (confirmed via the Maps attribution link's own `ll`/`z` query params and the vector-tile requests' zoom parameter, which read `z=17`/`!1i17!` after a stop click and `z=15`/`!1i15!` after the fit-all button), and clicking a different stop moved the camera to an entirely different location (genuine stop-to-stop hopping). The map chip trigger could not be demonstrated live on this specific test plan because its two stops sit close enough together that the chip renders underneath the strip's card row — a pre-existing, already-documented characteristic (`CLAUDE.md`'s "Open gaps": "Chips can tuck under an expanded card when two venues are <~300m apart"), not something this feature introduced; the chip wires to the identical `selectAndFocusStop` handler as the card, and its automated e2e coverage (using an unobstructed chip) passed.

## Feature: the transient banner auto-dismisses; a refusal or error never does
Goal: a success/info banner (a swap, a removal, a mode switch, a reroute all landing fine) should fade out on its own after a few seconds instead of sitting there until the next action; a refusal or error banner must keep behaving exactly as before — visible until the user does something else, because they need to actually read why something failed.
What should be done: find the ONE distinguishing signal that separates "this banner is a refusal" from "this banner is a success/info note", arm a 7s-visible + short-fade auto-dismiss for the latter only, cancel it outright for the former, pause it on hover/focus so a banner mid-read can't vanish under someone, restart a fresh interval when a new banner replaces an old one, respect reduced motion by keeping the timing but dropping the fade, and get the timer cleanup right — a stray `setTimeout` outliving the state it was armed for is the classic way this kind of feature breaks.
What was done (technical): the investigation found `bannerFlat` (the flag that already exists, picking the grey-vs-chartreuse left border) does **not** cleanly mean "refusal" — it is also `true` for three purely informational notes: the initial-plan window-overrun trim, an adapted-venue substitution, and a declined end-time-push confirmation. All three read as "the plan succeeded, here's what changed", not a failure. So auto-dismiss keys on a NEW, explicit `bannerPersist` boolean rather than reusing or deriving from `bannerFlat`, set alongside every one of the 11 `setBanner(...)` call sites in `page.tsx`: `true` at exactly the four mutation-refusal arms (`fireDisruption`'s `!data.rerouted`, `doSwap`'s `!data.swapped` with no pending confirm, `doRemove`'s `!data.removed`, `doModeSwitch`'s `!data.switched`), `false` everywhere else — the four mutation successes, the two initial-plan notes, and the declined-push note.
The timer itself is `app/lib/bannerDismiss.ts`'s `createBannerDismissController`, a framework-agnostic factory (no React) holding two `setTimeout` handles in closure and exposing exactly `arm()`/`cancel()` — no separate pause/resume names, since a pause IS a cancel and a resume IS a fresh arm. `arm()` always cancels first, so calling it back to back or mid-countdown never double-fires and a fresh banner replacing an old one gets its own full interval for free. `page.tsx` lazily constructs ONE controller instance per component lifetime (`useRef`, initialized once in the render body) whose callbacks close over `setBannerFading`/`setBanner`, and a `useEffect` keyed on `[banner, bannerPersist]` arms it when a banner is truthy and not persistent, cancels it otherwise, and — the load-bearing line — cancels it again in the cleanup on every dependency change AND on unmount, so a timer armed for one banner can never fire against a later one. Hover (`onMouseEnter`/`onMouseLeave`) and keyboard focus (`onFocus`/`onBlur`) on the banner call `cancel()`/`arm()` the same way; a pause that lands mid-fade also resets `bannerFading` to false, rescuing the banner back to full opacity rather than letting an already-started fade run to completion underneath a reading pointer.
CSS: `.banner--fading { opacity: 0; transition: opacity 0.3s ease; }` in `globals.css`, declared after `.banner--show` so it wins the opacity tie at equal specificity, giving the fade-out its own ~300ms duration distinct from the entrance's 0.5s. Reduced motion keeps the exact same JS timing (the `setTimeout`s don't know or care about the media query) and only strips the transition: `.banner--fading { transition: none !important; }` inside the existing `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)` block, so the banner snaps straight to invisible instead of easing there — mirrored on the existing convention (`TasteSurvey`'s confirmation auto-closes the same way: same timer, motion-only difference in CSS).
Tests: `bannerDismiss.test.ts` (6 cases) proves the timer/cleanup arithmetic without a DOM, using real-but-tiny delays (60ms) with generous margins the way `clientFetch.test.ts` already does for its own timeout tests — fade-then-dismiss in order once each, a cancel before the delay stops the fade forever, a cancel mid-fade stops the dismiss from landing, a fresh `arm()` cancels and restarts the prior countdown, back-to-back `arm()` calls never double-fire, and `cancel()` with nothing pending is a no-op. The persist-vs-auto-dismiss GATE itself lives entirely in `page.tsx` and is only e2e-reachable: new `banner-dismiss.spec.ts` (2 mock e2e) reuses remove's own success/refusal paths (removing one of two stops succeeds; removing the last stop is refused) to prove a success banner disappears within 9s and a refusal banner is still there after 8s. **A live revert-run was performed on the actual guard**: temporarily changed the effect's condition from `banner && !bannerPersist` to plain `banner` (letting a refusal arm the timer too) — the "stays until the next action" e2e test went red exactly as expected (the refusal banner vanished and the later assertion found nothing), then the guard was restored and both tests went green again.
Verified: `npm run check` fully green with no exclusions — lint 0 errors with the same 8 long-standing warnings, typecheck clean, 57 unit suites (56 existing + `bannerDismiss.test.ts`), webpack production build clean, and **119/119 mock e2e** (117 existing + 2 new), all three clock-sensitive window scenarios included and passing on this run. Not live-eyeballed against the real model on :3200 in this pass (a UI-timing change with no pipeline/data dependency); the owner's checklist is hover-pause behavior, the reduced-motion path, and watching the fade itself, none of which a fixture run can show.

## Fix: remove em-dashes from all user-facing text, plus the slogan and title
Goal: no em-dash anywhere the user reads it — static UI copy or model-generated prose — fix the two known mojibake instances if any survive, replace the "life moves simpler" slogan with "time to leave", and de-em-dash the app title.
What should be done: sweep the whole app/ tree for em-dashes, en-dashes-as-separators, and the two known UTF-8-em-dash mojibake corruptions (CP437's "ΓÇö", CP1252's "â€""), grouped by source — static UI string, LLM prompt, code comment (leave), mojibake. Fix every static string (comma or restructure, never a mechanical swap that reads wrong). Add a "don't use an em-dash" instruction to every system prompt that produces user-facing prose, without touching the prompts' own tuned wording otherwise. Add a shared sanitizer as the safety net for when a model ignores that instruction anyway, scoped to prose fields only. Swap the slogan and de-em-dash the title. Guard against regressions with a real test, not just a one-time sweep.
What was done (technical): the sweep found 1516 raw em-dash hits across 119 files, and a purpose-built comment-stripping classifier (a column-range tokenizer distinguishing `//`/`/* */` comments from real string/JSX content — this codebase's own prose-heavy comment style is why the raw count was so high) cut that to 293 real candidates, 126 of them outside test files. **No mojibake was found anywhere in the repo, in either known encoding** — the "known instance" the investigation named (page.tsx's remove-stop adapt reason) was already a clean literal em-dash, not corrupted; it got the same comma fix as everything else, not a corruption fix.
**Source A (71 static strings across 17 files) fixed directly**, comma as the default (matches this app's already-comma-heavy plain-voice banner style — CLAUDE.md's own examples read the same way), with three deliberate exceptions: a title/tagline separator became a colon (`layout.tsx`'s new title), two placeholder VALUES (the weather chip's missing-temperature dash, history's empty-stops-list dash) became words ("N/A", "No stops") since an em-dash there was never punctuation joining clauses, it was standing in for "no data" the way a comma cannot. One fix (`page.tsx`'s "Skip — just plan it" button) is an ACCESSIBLE NAME three e2e call sites match by exact string (`helpers.ts`'s `dismissClarifyIfShown`, `scenarios.spec.ts`); all three were updated to "Skip, just plan it" in the same pass, or the button would have kept working while every test that dismisses clarify silently stopped finding it.
**Source B (the three LLM system prompts — `PLANNER_SYSTEM_PROMPT`, swap's `REFINE_SYSTEM`, `selectVenues`' `SYSTEM_PROMPT`) got exactly one line added each and nothing else touched**, per the explicit instruction not to disturb the most-tuned surface in the app: "Never use an em dash (—) or en dash as punctuation; use a comma, or write it as two sentences" (or the planner's equivalent naming its own three prose fields). The instruction itself avoids using an em-dash, deliberately — telling a model not to use one while using one to say it undercuts the point. **A prompt instruction alone is not reliably obeyed**, which is why there's also a sanitizer: new `app/lib/sanitizeProse.ts`, a pure `sanitizeModelProse(text)` that replaces any em/en-dash (plus the two mojibake forms, on the belt-and-suspenders theory that a provider hop could misencode one even though nothing in this app's own request path does) with a comma and cleans up the seams a mechanical swap leaves (a leading dash's stray comma, a comma landing next to terminal punctuation, doubled spaces). **Wired at exactly the two places raw model JSON becomes the typed objects every downstream consumer reads** — `selectVenues()`'s `Selection.reason` (the "why here" text; every consumer, including a swap's `findReplacement` re-pick, inherits the sanitized value for free, since they all read the one `Selection` object this function returns) and `coercePlan()`'s `PlannedActivity.intent`, `PlannerQuestion.question`/`.options[]`, and `TimeIntent.label` (CLAUDE.md's own words: "a display label only"). Deliberately NOT wired onto `searchQuery`, `category_signals`, `constraints`, `aesthetic`, or any venue identity field (name, id) — those are structured inputs to a Places search or hard-matched against `normalizeConstraint`, not sentences a person reads, and the task was explicit that a "—" there might be load-bearing data this function has no business rewriting.
**The slogan and title turned out to be one string.** `layout.tsx`'s `<title>` was `"Itinerary — life moves simpler."`, now `"Itinerary: time to leave."` — both fixes land on the same line. The VISIBLE hero tagline is a separate DOM node, `page.tsx`'s `<div className="empty__sub">`, with no em-dash of its own; it became `time to leave.` to match.
**Fallout, found the reliable way — by running every test rather than trusting the sweep.** Every `.test.ts` was run individually (the shared runner stops at the first failing suite, which would have hidden later ones): two rounds surfaced eleven exact-text assertions pinned against the OLD em-dash strings (`swap.test.ts` ×2, `planGuards.test.ts` ×9 — the second round found six the first grep pass missed, different wording from parametrized cases), all updated to match. The same discipline caught three more at the e2e layer that no unit test could see: `failloud.spec.ts`'s five exact-text fail-loud pins (one shared constant covers two cases), and `recovery.spec.ts`'s weather-gate regex. **Two of my own new em-dash e2e tests failed on their first run for an unrelated reason** (missing a `dismissClarifyIfShown` call the existing recovery tests already knew to make) — fixed, not a production issue.
Tests: `sanitizeProse.test.ts` (15 cases — the comma swap, tight/spaced dashes, multiple dashes, a leading dash, a dash before/after terminal punctuation, double-space collapse, both mojibake forms for both dash types, non-string/empty-string safety, the list variant). `emdashGuard.test.ts` — the creep-back guard: walks every non-test `.ts`/`.tsx` file, strips comments with the same column-range logic the sweep used, and fails on any em/en-dash-as-punctuation outside a small, individually-commented allowlist (the three prompt bodies wholesale; two dash-matching regexes that parse PROVIDER text, not author it; four dev-only log/error strings that never reach a user; two legitimate en-dash time RANGES, which are an en-dash's actual job and never in scope). A second case sanity-checks the allowlist itself still matches something, so a stale entry can't silently stop protecting anything. **Both directions of the guard were proven with a live revert-run**: a real em-dash injected into a fixed banner string was caught with the exact file and line, then reverted; a fresh comment-only em-dash was confirmed to NOT trip it, then reverted — proving the comment strip is neither toothless nor trigger-happy. `e2e/emdash.spec.ts` adds 7 mock e2e checking the RENDERED surface directly (the landing hero, a planned strip, a fail-loud message, a swap banner, a remove banner, both recovery panels) for the literal character, the belt to the guard's suspenders.
Verified: lint 0 errors with the same 8 long-standing warnings, typecheck clean, 56 unit suites (54 existing + the 2 new), webpack production build clean, and 117/117 mock e2e (110 existing + 7 new), all three known clock-sensitive scenarios included and passing. Live-verified on :3200 against the REAL model (not fixtures; port 3000 untouched): the title read `Itinerary: time to leave.` in the tab and the DOM; a real plan's "why here" text for **Harriet's Rooftop** came back as "...with its high rating and live music, it's also relatively close to the starting point." — a comma sitting exactly where a model reaches for an em-dash, which is either the prompt instruction or the sanitizer actually doing something, not merely a lucky response; a live "somewhere cheaper" swap produced "Swapped restaurant, Earls Kitchen + Bar is a great choice for a lively night out..." (code-level fix and model-sanitizer both visible in one banner); a live removal produced "Removed Earls Kitchen + Bar - Financial District (King & University) and moved the later stops earlier." with the venue's own real hyphenated Places name correctly left untouched, confirming the sanitizer's prose-only scope holds in practice. No em-dash anywhere on any screen touched.

## Feature (drive-vs-transit mode, Stage 2 of 2): switch an EXISTING plan between transit and driving
Goal: let someone who already has a plan change how the whole day travels — transit ↔ driving — and have the plan genuinely re-route in the new mode, with every venue they chose still in it.
What should be done: reuse remove-stop's cascade rather than reroute's, since "re-price every leg from the first movable stop, keep every venue" is exactly `planAnchorInbound` + `resettleTail` minus the splice; guarantee in BOTH directions that a switch can change a stop's time but never its venue; give it its own module and its own endpoint on the remove route's template; re-price only what is still ahead of the floor; make it re-price travel and nothing else — no Places call, no model call; and put a toggle on the existing-plan view that shows the current mode and a pending state while the re-route runs.
What was done (technical): new `app/api/itinerary/modeSwitch.ts` — `switchTravelMode(itinerary, target, now, deps)`. It clones, derives statuses, takes `floorTime`, finds the FIRST MOVABLE timed stop (not locked, not at or before the floor), re-prices that stop's inbound leg through `planAnchorInbound`, resolves its start, hands the rest to `resettleTail`, and commits with one `Object.assign`. No new cascade, no new travel arithmetic, no new availability rule; `swap.ts` and `removeStop.ts` are untouched except for the two additions below, and their suites (81 + 14 + 18 cases) are green unchanged.
**The deps are bound to the TARGET mode, and that one line is the whole feature.** Swap, remove and reroute all bind `realDeps(itinerary.travelMode)` — they inherit how the plan travels. This mutation IS the change to how it travels, so it binds `realDeps(target)`. Binding the stored mode would re-price the entire day in the mode being left and then relabel the plan, which is the silent failure the stage exists to prevent.
**THE LOAD-BEARING RULE IS "SWITCHING MODE MAY CHANGE EVERY STOP'S TIME; IT MUST NEVER CHANGE ANY STOP'S VENUE", and it needs TWO guards because a switch moves stops in both directions.** Transit → driving is faster and pulls stops EARLIER, where a venue that has not opened yet reads as unusable and `resettleTail` answers by REPLACING it — remove-stop's `clampEarlierToAvailability` already solves exactly that, so a switch turns it ON and the stop holds at its own opening time with its venue intact. Driving → transit is slower and pushes stops LATER, where a venue that has CLOSED is replaced just the same and no clamp reaches it. That half needed a second, net-new guard: `resettleTail` gained `neverReplaceVenue` (a 15th parameter, defaulting to false, so swap and removeStop are byte-identical to before it existed), which refuses at the top of the unusable branch. It refuses BEFORE the `findReplacement` loop rather than discarding its result, and that ordering is not tidiness — `findReplacement` spends a Places search and a model call, and a mode switch is DEFINED as re-pricing travel without ever re-selecting a venue. A test asserts zero `searchPools`, `selectVenues` and `interpret` calls across a successful switch.
The other decisions worth recording. The departure from home is HELD (read via `homeDeparture` before the new leg overwrites `homeLeg`), so a faster vehicle changes when you ARRIVE rather than silently rewriting when you left; the anchor's start is then `max(held departure + new leg, floor)`, clamped upward to its own opening time. Only what is AHEAD of the floor re-prices — a mid-outing switch leaves already-travelled legs in the mode they were actually travelled in, which is the Stage 1 invariant holding rather than a gap. Switching TO transit DELETES `travelMode` rather than writing `"transit"`, because absent is what transit means; and since `Object.assign` copies keys but cannot remove one, that delete is replayed on the real object after the commit — without it a plan switched to transit would still read as driving to every later mutation, and a test pins `hasOwnProperty` false. A stated `plannedEndISO` the re-timed day now runs past is a NOTE in the banner, not a confirm dialog like the swap engine's: a swap's push is a side effect the user never mentioned, whereas here the new end is the direct arithmetic of the choice they just made, so re-asking would be asking them to confirm their own request. The no-op (switching to the mode the plan is already in) is a refusal at both ends — the browser never sends it, and the engine returns `switched: false`, which reaches the route as `changed: false`, so no CAS runs and no version is bumped.
`POST /api/itinerary/[id]/mode` is a sibling of the remove route on the same template — same id shape, same rate limit, same `updateItinerary` CAS with `expectedVersion`, same version echo and ETag, same auth precedent (none beyond the plan id, as `/swap` and `/remove`).
