It differs in its body, which is why it is its own file: it names a travel mode and no stop at all. `travelMode` is REQUIRED here and validated through the shared `parseOptionalTravelMode` allowlist with a required-ness check on top. `parseModePayload` mirrors it in `clientPayloads.ts`.
UI: the topbar gained the same `radiogroup` the landing pill carries, so the control the mode was chosen with is the control it is changed with. It shows the CURRENT mode as the checked half before offering the other; both halves disable and the target pulses with `aria-busy` and an `sr-only` live region while the re-route is in flight; the pending half is deliberately NOT pre-checked, because the switch can still be refused. `doModeSwitch` is shaped like `doRemove` — one operation lock, the version echo, the ambiguous-outcome refresh — and reuses the id-keyed reflow unchanged, which works here for free precisely because a mode switch is the one mutation guaranteed never to change a venue id. On success the LANDING toggle follows the plan, so a Replan (and the empty screen after this plan ends) carries the mode the user last actually chose instead of silently reverting. `TransitIcon` was exported from `ItineraryStrip` so the control uses the same bus/car glyphs the plan's own legs are drawn with. Adding a fifth control to the topbar broke the 320px layout — measured at 39.8px against the 44px target the mobile suite holds every `.topbar` control to — so at ≤560px the mode labels go VISUALLY hidden rather than `display: none` (the accessible name has to survive, and `getByRole("radio", {name})` is how the specs find them) and the gap plus Replan's and End's side padding tighten; every control keeps its 44px minimum and all four tested widths pass.
Tests: `modeSwitch.test.ts` (18 cases) splits deliberately in two. The MODE-BINDING cases inject every dep EXCEPT `getSingleLeg`, so the leg fetcher comes from `realDeps(target)` — under `E2E_MOCK` the fixture builder — which covers the whole chain at once (requested mode → `realDeps` → `mockSwapDeps` → `mockLeg`) and is the only shape that can prove a switch re-prices rather than relabels. The SCHEDULING cases inject legs too, because the clamp, the floor and the refusals are arithmetic. `mode-switch.spec.ts` adds 5 mock e2e that read the strip's venue names before and after every switch and require them identical. The required revert-runs were performed as a matrix, since the two guards cover different directions: with BOTH off the clamp case reproduces the production bug exactly (`b1` → `bar_fresh`, the bar silently substituted) and the closing-time case switches instead of refusing; with only the clamp off the switch is refused rather than substituting, which shows `neverReplaceVenue` backstopping; with only `neverReplaceVenue` off the closing-time case substitutes while the clamp case still passes. Both were restored and the file verified byte-identical.
Verified: the FINAL `npm run check` was fully green with NO exclusions — lint 0 errors with the same 8 long-standing warnings, typecheck clean, 54 unit suites, webpack production build (with `/api/itinerary/[id]/mode` registered dynamic), and **110/110 mock e2e**, all three clock-sensitive window scenarios included. An earlier run at 20:44 was 108/110, and the two failures were the documented pair (`a stated window plans multiple stops that end inside it`, `an OVER-STUFFED window drops what doesn't fit and says so`) with the expected clamp symptom ("Couldn't fit anything into your 5-9pm window once travel time is counted"); they REPRODUCED IDENTICALLY at HEAD with every change stashed, including untracked files, one minute later at 20:38. By the 21:00 run their stated windows had rolled to TOMORROW instead of being clamped and all three passed — which is the other half of the standing proof that the clock, not the code, decides those three. Live-verified through the real route and store on :3200 in mock mode (port 3000 untouched): a transit plan switched to driving re-priced the home leg from a 16-minute `505 Fixture` ride to a 17-minute drive, held the 8:00 PM departure, moved both stops by a minute with the strike-through reflow, kept both venue ids, and stored `travelMode: "driving"` at version 2; switching back restored the ride and its badge, restored the original times, and REMOVED the `travelMode` key entirely. A mid-outing switch (simulated 9:00 PM, dinner active) left the locked stop and the already-travelled home leg untouched and re-priced only the leg ahead. The refusal paths were exercised against the live endpoint: server-side no-op and a finished day both return `switched: false` with no version bump, a stale `version` 409s, and an unknown or missing `travelMode` 400s. NO live-provider verification was performed: real drive times, real road geometry and the Routes billing line remain the owner's live pass.
Stated, not fixed (inherited from Stage 1): reroute's `excludeTransit` / `transit_cancelled` disruption has no driving meaning, so a plan switched INTO driving inherits having no reachable disruption trigger. Also deliberately not covered by mock e2e: the clamp itself, because the fixture drive is a minute LONGER than the fixture transit ride, so no fixture switch pulls a stop earlier — that case lives in the engine suite, where leg lengths are chosen to the minute.

## Fix (drive-vs-transit mode, Stage 1): every driving leg was silently priced as a WALK, because the DRIVE request was illegal
Goal: make a driving plan actually get driving times and road geometry from the provider, instead of falling through to walking on every leg — and make the next upstream rejection of this kind readable in one log line rather than costing a whole stage.
What should be done: find why a DRIVE leg never priced, fix the request that Google was rejecting, gate that fix so the transit and walk request bodies stay byte-identical, stop the catch from throwing away the one field that would have explained it, and close the test gap that let a request the provider REFUSES pass a full `npm run check`.
What was done (technical): the root cause is a rule of Routes v2 that nothing here knew: **`travelMode: "DRIVE"` defaults to `routingPreference: TRAFFIC_UNAWARE`, and a `departureTime` is ILLEGAL under it** — the API answers 400 INVALID_ARGUMENT, "Timestamp cannot be set for TRAFFIC_UNAWARE routing mode". Every driving leg carries a departureTime (each leg is priced at its own accumulated departure, from the 2026-07-18 §1.5 fix), so **every DRIVE request 400'd**. `computeRoute` swallows a per-mode failure by design, so it returned null; `buildDrivingLeg` did exactly what it is written to do with a dead drive and fell through to its WALK arm; and a 30 km drive was scheduled as a ~371-minute walk. Nothing was broken in `buildDrivingLeg`, the scheduler, the parse or the strip — the request never got far enough to produce a number.
The fix is one conditional in `computeRoute`'s body builder: where a future `departureTime` is attached, a DRIVE also gets `routingPreference: "TRAFFIC_AWARE"`. **It is gated on DRIVE in BOTH directions, and that is load-bearing rather than tidy** — the same live probe that proved the DRIVE rejection also proved the inverse, that WALK and TRANSIT reject a routing preference outright ("Routing preference cannot be set for WALK or BICYCLE", "... for TRANSIT"). Leaking it one mode sideways would 400 both transit requests and downgrade every transit plan the same silent way. TRAFFIC_AWARE and deliberately not TRAFFIC_AWARE_OPTIMAL: both accept the timestamp and both fix the 400, but OPTIMAL escalates the call to the Routes **Preferred** SKU, so the cheaper one that still reads traffic is the right answer and the choice is commented at the site. Dropping `departureTime` for DRIVE was the rejected alternative — it also 200s, but it re-opens "every leg priced at the outing start" and throws away traffic awareness for nothing. `buildLeg`, `buildDrivingLeg`, `getSingleLeg`'s dispatch and the scheduler are untouched; this is purely the request body.
Second, the diagnostic hole that made a one-line bug cost a stage: `computeRoute`'s catch was `catch {}` and logged `routes_mode_unavailable` with the travelMode alone, so a 400 (our own request is malformed) was indistinguishable from a provider outage. `ProviderError.failure.upstreamStatus` was already modelled and already carried it — the catch now binds the error and logs that STATUS. The upstream BODY is still never logged; the "never log the upstream body" rule is about bodies, and the status is the field the rule exists to make safe to keep. An absent status still means the call never reached the provider (timeout/transport, where `fetchProvider` throws with no `failure`), and `JSON.stringify` drops the key rather than printing null.
Third, the test gap, which is the part worth remembering: `driveMode.test.ts`'s `recordModes` stubs fetch with a canned 200 and asserts `body.travelMode === "DRIVE"` — it proved what we ASKED for, never that the ask was legal, so a request the provider refuses passed lint, typecheck, 53 unit suites, the build and 105 e2e. Added `recordBodies` (captures the full parsed request body per mode) and a `strict` responder that rejects the way the real API rejects, both rules transcribed from the live probe. Four new cases: routingPreference is present and exactly `TRAFFIC_AWARE` on a DRIVE carrying a future departureTime (with the Preferred-SKU pin as its own assertion); it never reaches WALK or TRANSIT, and a transit plan's request bodies still carry exactly `origin`/`destination`/`travelMode`/`departureTime` — byte-identity in the strongest form available offline; against the enforcing stub a driving leg still prices as a drive with real minutes and geometry; and the same stub leaves a transit plan priced as transit. Revert-run both ways: deleting the conditional turns the presence case red AND reproduces the production bug in the end-to-end case ("not the walk fallback"), while leaking it to all modes turns the two transit/walk cases red instead. `driveMode.test.ts` is 20 cases, up from 16; suite count is unchanged at 53.
Verified live through the shipped code path (`getSingleLeg`, not a hand-written body), Richmond Hill → downtown Toronto departing in an hour: `mode: "driving"`, 58 min raw + the 10 min policy margin = 68 min over 38.7 km, with a 1049-character road polyline — and the same call on a transit plan still returns `mode: "transit"`, 86 + 5 = 91 min, so nothing leaked. The traffic preference is doing real work rather than merely satisfying the validator: the identical route requested without a departureTime (free-flow TRAFFIC_UNAWARE) comes back 45 min against the traffic-aware 58.

## Feature (drive-vs-transit mode, Stage 1 of 2): a landing toggle picks how the whole day travels
Goal: let the user choose DRIVE or TRANSIT before planning, build the entire plan in that mode, keep every later mutation in it, and render driving legs correctly — without building the live mid-plan switch, which is Stage 2 on a separate branch.
What should be done: store the mode on the itinerary as an optional field where absent means transit (so no plan needs migrating); make the leg fetcher request DRIVE instead of TRANSIT on a driving plan; give driving its own relabel threshold and margin as clearly LABELLED POLICY rather than measurements; make swap, remove and reroute inherit the stored mode by binding it once where their deps are built; fix the strip's leg card, which had no driving branch and therefore rendered a drive as a walk; draw a driving leg's real provider road line and never a straight one; and add a toggle to the landing controls that involves no LLM and reads nothing from the prompt.
What was done (technical): `Itinerary.travelMode?: "transit" | "driving"` joins `timeZone`/`plannedEndISO` as an optional stored fact — `createItinerary` never writes it out for transit, so a transit plan stays byte-identical to a pre-drive-mode one and every existing stored plan reads as transit with no migration. `parseOptionalTravelMode` mirrors `parseOptionalTimeZone` on the server, `clientPayloads` mirrors it in the browser (still duplicated, since it cannot import server modules), and both leg-mode allowlists gained `"driving"` — the two gates that would otherwise have rejected a driving plan on write and on read. The create route and `POST /api/schedule/travel` both accept and thread it.
`travel.ts` gained `PlanTravelMode`, `DRIVING_MARGIN_MIN = 10` and `DRIVING_SHORT_LEG_WALK_METERS = 700`, both commented as deliberate policy estimates and neither sourced from any data: the margin is ~5 minutes to get moving plus ~5 to park and walk in, and the threshold is higher than transit's 400 m because a short drive is a real drive whose park-and-approach overhead swamps it, where a short "transit" route is Google walking the hop internally. A real parking data source replaces the constant, the same shape as the `isUsableAt` reservation seam. `buildDrivingLeg` is a SEPARATE pure function rather than a flag inside `buildLeg`, so the transit relabel ladder is untouched: a priced drive at or past the threshold becomes `mode: "driving"` with the provider's DRIVE duration plus the margin; under it, it relabels to WALK **only when the WALK route actually priced**, because a short drive's duration is a car's and presenting it as a walk's would invent a fact; a failed drive with a usable walk becomes a walk. `getSingleLeg` takes the plan mode as a trailing parameter and dispatches, requesting DRIVE + WALK on a driving plan (the walk so a short hop can still relabel) and TRANSIT + WALK otherwise. **The plan-level mode is an INTENT, not a per-leg guarantee — a driving plan legitimately contains walk legs**, and that is now recorded as an invariant in `CLAUDE.md`.
The three engines are one line each, exactly at the seam the investigation predicted: `realDeps(itinerary.travelMode)` at `swap.ts`, `removeStop.ts` and `reroute.ts` (the itinerary rather than `work`, which is not constructed until after the call — same value, in scope earlier). No mode parameter was threaded through any `deps.getSingleLeg(...)` call site. The mock builders take the mode too (`mockSwapDeps`, `mockRerouteDeps`, `mockTravelLegs`, `mockLeg`); without that a driving e2e would have passed while every fixture leg routed as transit.
Display: `TransitIcon` gained a CAR, and the strip's leg card gained an EXPLICIT driving branch — the bug it fixes is that the card is a ternary whose final `else` is the WALK arm, so a drive rendered as "walk" with the pedestrian-safety caution under a BUS glyph, and TypeScript could not catch it because the else makes the ternary total. A driving leg shows the car, the word "Drive", the total and leave·arrive, with no badges, no timeline, no transfer rows and no walking caution; the transit decorations already degraded correctly and were not touched. `routeSelectable` now admits driving, so a driving leg is a native `aria-pressed` button (it drives map focus and the duration pointer, and invents no `aria-expanded`). `displayableRouteMode` and `travelLegVisibility` admit `"driving"`, and the pre-start home exception now covers a vehicular home leg — transit or driving — so a fresh driving plan shows its route from creation. The map gained a third styling case: solid ink at the same weight as a whole-leg walk, reached only from decoded provider geometry, and a driving leg with NO geometry draws NOTHING rather than inheriting the walk branch's endpoint fallback, because a straight line between two venues is not a road. `legDetail` gives a driving leg the map's duration label ("Drive · N min") at the existing leg midpoint. `pathSegments` was not touched: `pathMode()` returns null for a DRIVE step, so a driving leg carries none, which is correct.
The unpriceable case is the one place this deviates from the app's existing habit, and it is deliberate and flagged: when BOTH DRIVE and WALK fail on a driving plan the leg stays `mode: "unknown"` with NO minutes, rather than taking the crow-flies walk-speed estimate the transit path uses. That estimate is 3-6x a car's time and nothing measured it. The cost is real and named in the code: an unknown leg carries zero minutes, so the schedule does not pad for it — the §6.2 hazard the transit fallback exists to close. It requires the provider to fail on both modes for the same hop, and the transit fallback is untouched (a regression test pins it).
The toggle is a fourth labelled section in the existing landing pill — a `radiogroup` with Transit/Drive, styled from the existing tokens, defaulting to transit. No LLM, nothing inferred from the prompt. Adding it narrowed the other sections enough that "Starting location" wrapped to two lines and pushed its own input 6.36 px below its neighbours, which the 768 px mobile spec caught as a 6.5 px alignment failure; measured in a real browser and fixed with `white-space: nowrap` on `.prompt__label`, re-measured at 0 px spread with no collision against the go button.
Tests: `driveMode.test.ts` (16 cases — the allowlist, the margin and threshold as pinned policy constants, the relabel both sides of the boundary, the short-drive-without-walk-data case, DRIVE-vs-TRANSIT request assertions in both directions, and both outage cases) and `driveModeEngines.test.ts` (7 cases). The engines suite deliberately does NOT stub `getSingleLeg`: it runs under `E2E_MOCK` and injects every other dep, so the leg fetcher must come from `realDeps(itinerary.travelMode)` and the whole chain — stored mode → `realDeps` → mock builder → `mockLeg` — is under test at once. Both validator suites gained mode cases (driving valid, absent valid, unknown refused, a driving leg beside a walk leg in one plan). `drive-mode.spec.ts` adds 5 mock e2e; the map harness gained a driving specimen and `maps-resilience.spec.ts` two more.
Both required revert-runs were performed and restored. Removing the driving strip branch turned four driving cases red with exactly the Gate-4 symptom — `expect(locator).toHaveText` "Drive", received "walk" — while the map-line and selectability cases correctly stayed green, since neither depends on that branch. Breaking the mock mode-threading (dropping `planMode` from the three `mockLeg` call sites) turned 4 of the 5 driving e2e red and 2 of the 7 engine cases red, with the transit cases still passing — the exact "routes as transit while claiming to drive" failure the threading exists to prevent.
`buildSchedule` is empty-diff: it still runs on `leg.totalMinutes` alone, which is why the driving margin lives inside `buildLeg` and not in the scheduler. `occupancyMinutes` was left untouched — the stop's buffer and the leg's margin share a word and are different policies. Reroute's `excludeTransit` / `transit_cancelled` disruption has no driving meaning and is inert on a driving plan; a driving plan has no reachable disruption trigger in Stage 1, which is stated in the code and not fixed here. Full `npm run check` green: lint 0 errors with the same 8 long-standing warnings, typecheck clean, 53 unit suites, webpack production build, and 105/105 mock e2e across 13 spec files — including all three known clock-sensitive scenarios, which passed on this run. NO live-provider verification was performed: real driving geometry, real drive times and the Routes DRIVE billing line are unmeasured and remain the owner's live pass.

## Fix (complete travel-leg visibility): hide future inter-stop walks and show pre-start home transit
Goal: keep the map focused on the complete travel leg that is relevant now, so the first stop-to-second stop WALK cannot appear before the outing begins while the real home-to-first TRANSIT remains visible from plan creation until the first stop starts.
What should be done: apply one exact-ID visibility decision to identified whole WALK and TRANSIT legs and every native object they own; prioritize an underway leg, then pre-start home TRANSIT, then the active stop's outbound leg; allow one manually selected inter-stop WALK or TRANSIT without duplicating the automatic leg; preserve home WALK, identity-absent WALK, legacy transit, provider geometry, palette, cleanup, bounds, markers, scheduling and timezone behavior; and prove the boundary, interaction, lifecycle and regression cases through the pure seam and real map/strip harness.
What was done (technical): `travelLegVisibility.ts` now owns mode-neutral automatic selection, exact automatic/manual deduplication, toggle/replacement and stale-ID retention plus the shared map predicate. Its priority is the first identified WALK/TRANSIT whose existing scheduler window is underway, otherwise identified home TRANSIT while finite `displayNow` is strictly before the authoritative first-stop start, otherwise the active stop's identified WALK/TRANSIT outbound leg. The home exception therefore includes time before the calculated departure, hands off to the normal half-open underway window at departure, and ends exactly at first-stop start; missing or invalid timing invents no boundary. The page projects the home leg and stop legs into that seam, keeps one ephemeral manual ID across ordinary refreshes only while the exact leg survives, and clears it on plan replacement. The strip gives identified inter-stop WALK summaries the same native `aria-pressed` button contract as TRANSIT without inventing a transit timeline; home WALK remains static. `ItineraryMap` tags each leg as home or inter-stop and calls the same predicate before decoding geometry or constructing native objects, then reuses it for route labels/bubbles and transfer markers, so a hidden complete leg creates no whole WALK, ride, embedded walk, changed halo, label/bubble or transfer marker. The focused unit seam passed 20/20 and the real-component map suite passed 17/17, including exact decoded and endpoint-fallback WALK paths, home-before-departure and exact-start boundaries, pointer/keyboard manual selection, deduplication, replacement, stale-ID retention, rerender/unmount/remount cleanup, changed halos and unchanged legacy/palette/dot/marker/bounds behavior. The required discriminating revert-run temporarily made identified inter-stop WALK always visible: the screenshot regression failed at `maps-resilience.spec.ts:602`, receiving one active `#2E6F8A` WALK polyline on the exact decoded path `[[43.649,-79.42],[43.6495,-79.4195],[43.65,-79.419]]` instead of none, while the independently named home-transit boundary case still passed; after restoring only that bypass, both cases passed 2/2. Final `npm run check` passed with lint at 0 errors and the same 8 warnings, typecheck clean, all 51 unit suites, the webpack production build and all 98 mock e2e tests across 12 files. After the complete gate, `next-env.d.ts` already had the committed `./.next/dev/types/routes.d.ts` import and no textual or staged diff; its remaining build/dev line-ending artifact was restored to the exact committed bytes, verified by matching filtered and raw hashes, and was not included in the change. No live-provider geometry or billing claim was made; coordinates, provider fallback and request fields are unchanged.

Post-review verification: an independent audit tightened only the plan/history harness fixture so the plan ID changes while the selected leg ID survives, making the explicit plan-identity reset—not stale-ID retention—the reason the assertion passes. The complete `npm run check` was then rerun on that exact final tree and passed again: lint 0 errors/8 warnings, typecheck, 51 unit suites, webpack production build and 98/98 mock e2e. All three known clock-sensitive cases passed on the first run. The final complete gate left `next-env.d.ts` byte-identical to `HEAD`, with its committed dev import, empty textual/staged diffs, and matching filtered and raw hashes, so no final restore was necessary; no build was run after that final verification.

## Feature (Transit B final): context-focused complete travel-leg visibility
Goal: show only the transit journey relevant to the traveller's current context by default, while allowing one completed or future complete leg to be inspected without losing the automatic leg.
What should be done: derive one automatic identified transit leg from the existing display clock (underway first, otherwise the active event's outbound leg); lift one ephemeral `manualLegId` shared by strip and map; carry `legId` through all home/inter-stop projections; filter an identified transit leg as one complete unit before any native map visual is built; keep coherent timeline behavior, keyboard/accessibility state, legacy all-leg rendering, and walking-only routes intact; clear stale or plan-crossing manual identities; preserve every palette, provider fact, coordinate, walk-style, lifecycle, mutation, persistence and scheduling invariant; leave Tasks 2–4 unstarted.
What was done (technical): `travelLegVisibility.ts` is the pure decision seam for automatic priority using the existing half-open `legUnderway()` windows, deduplicated automatic+manual visibility, toggle/replacement, legacy detection and stale-ID retention. `page.tsx` owns the non-persisted manual ID, resets it when the itinerary ID changes, retains it across ordinary status refreshes while the exact leg survives, and projects `legId` through home/inter-stop strip and map models. Identified transit summary buttons share that state with `aria-pressed`; only coherent timelines publish `aria-expanded`/`aria-controls`, underway timelines remain automatic, and legacy timelines retain local state. `ItineraryMap` filters complete identified transit legs before geometry decoding and applies the same predicate to labels/bubbles and transfer markers; walking-only legs bypass it, and the existing effect-local cleanup replaces/detaches owned overlays when visibility changes. Hidden legs therefore create no ride, embedded walk, halo, label/bubble or transfer marker; visible legs retain exact Part 2 colours and Piece 3 walking dots/coordinates. The focused harness uses fixed `2026-07-25` instants and proves route-specific native paths, embedded walks, labels/bubbles, real-coordinate transfers, keyboard toggling, replacement, deduplication and cleanup; legacy coverage stays in the existing all-leg specimen. The required revert-run temporarily bypassed only the pre-construction filter: the focused case failed as expected with two hidden slot-0 `#005A9C` overlays instead of zero, then passed after restoration. Tasks 2–4 were not started.

## Feature (transit ride palette series, Part 2): one occurrence colour across every ride line and badge
Goal: make separate transit ride occurrences visually distinguishable even when the provider assigns them the same route colour, while keeping every representation of one exact ride in agreement and making embedded walking gaps materially easier to see.
What should be done: resolve the persisted Part 1 `paletteSlot` through one deterministic 24-colour browser-safe table; apply that ride colour to native primaries, changed halos, map bubbles, compact badges and BOARD badges with white text; preserve provider colour/text metadata as legacy and explicit-overflow fallback; expose authentic route names for color-only map bubbles; strengthen only the approved walking-dot style; and leave geometry, matching, allocation, timing, mutations, bounds and markers alone.
What was done (technical): `transitRidePalette.ts` maps integer slots `0..23` to the fixed approved unique colours and returns no app colour for missing/null/invalid/overflow values; it never wraps, hashes, randomizes, consults provider facts or includes reserved `#C8F000`. Each transit path resolves its OWN slot before the existing provider/ink fallback, and the same resolved colour feeds both primary and same-colour changed halo. One `bubbleDisplayColors` helper resolves each fact record for the real map bubble and the shared strip `RouteBadge`, so compact and expanded BOARD uses cannot drift; app-coloured surfaces force `#FFFFFF`, while legacy and null-overflow records retain their prior provider background/text fallbacks. Paired map bubbles keep their visual stack and now publish ordered authentic route names through the existing screen-reader-only mechanism; IDs, source indices, leg IDs and slots remain absent from labels/attributes. Embedded transit-leg walk symbols changed only to neutral `#4F6F7E`, `12px` repeat, primary opacity `.88`/scale `2.0`/z `2`, and changed underlay `.24`/scale `3.6`/z `1`, with both base and symbol stroke transparent. The 2 m boundary tolerance, 0.5 m floor, merge/flush/order rules, exact native coordinates, ordinary solid walk legs, effect-local cleanup, bounds, markers and every nonvisual pipeline seam are unchanged. Exactly coincident native paths remain a known static-polyline limitation; coordinates are never offset. This completes Task 1 only—Tasks 2–4 were not started. Focused results: palette 3/3, bubbles 18/18, detail 33/33, ride identity 11/11, travel 28/28, server schema 18/18, browser payload 19/19, and the real-component mock map/strip harness 10/10. The required revert-run bypassed only map palette resolution: the key cross-surface case failed because provider red `#D71920` replaced expected slot-0 `#005A9C`, then passed 1/1 after restoration. Final gates: all 50 unit suites, 91/91 mock e2e, typecheck clean, lint 0 errors with the same 10 warnings, and webpack production build green. The built app on port 3200 returned HTTP 200, planned two mock-backed stops, reached map `ready`, rendered slot-0 blue/white identically in real strip and map bubbles, leaked no ride ID, and logged no browser warning/error. This is deterministic mock verification only; real-provider visual inspection remains Anshi's checklist.

## Plumbing (transit ride palette series, Part 1): persisted exact-ride identity and palette slots, with no visual change
Goal: establish a safe app-owned contract linking each provider transit ride's optional geometry to its exact facts/bubble record before Part 2 changes any colours, without guessing across independently filtered arrays or changing the visible product.
What should be done: give every fresh travel leg and source transit occurrence persisted opaque identity; copy one occurrence bundle to whichever facts and geometry records survive; reserve and allocate unique palette slots `0..23` in home-to-first then inter-stop/provider order; preserve untouched identities through persistence and mutations; leave legacy all-absent metadata loadable; make overflow explicit; and prove that filtering on either side cannot shift a later ride.
What was done (technical): `TravelLeg` gained optional `legId`; identified `TransitSummary` and transit `PathSegment` records share `rideId`, raw flattened `sourceStepIndex`, and `paletteSlot`. `readSteps` creates one ride identity at the top of the provider-step iteration, before facts and geometry admission diverge, and the only exported extraction seam returns both arrays from that single traversal. IDs use `crypto.randomUUID()` behind a narrow injectable factory; they are app metadata, never provider/LLM facts or user-visible labels. A topology allocator first reserves every retained valid slot, then assigns each new occurrence the lowest unused slot in home→inter-stop/provider order; exactly 24 rides receive `0..23`, and later rides remain valid with explicit `null` rather than wrapping, hashing, or choosing a colour. New identity generations are atomic and relationally checked: compatibility copies, facts, and paths must agree by exact ride/source/slot tuple; ride, leg, source, and non-null slot collisions are rejected, while the established server policy may drop only a malformed decorative path. Legacy records with every new field absent remain valid and are never backfilled or paired by filtered position. Initial mock/live legs allocate as a complete topology; JSON persistence preserves the metadata; and successful swap/reroute/remove flows allocate at the existing `rebuildLegs` seam, so untouched legs retain IDs/slots while recomputed legs receive fresh IDs and the first free slots. The deferred `stops[].travelToNext` server-sanitizer gap was deliberately not broadened. Presentation code only carries the extra optional facts fields; no renderer reads them, so provider line/badge/text colours, walking dots, overlay geometry/cleanup, labels, timings, prompts, marker positions, scheduler arithmetic, and mutation boundaries are unchanged. Focused coverage proves shared identity across facts/geometry, asymmetric omissions without ordinal drift, provider-colour independence, travel order, retained-slot reservation, 24-plus-overflow behavior, atomic/relational server and browser validation, JSON reload, all leg modes, legacy absence, mutation preservation/replacement, UI view-model text, and unchanged timing/geometry facts. The required revert-run temporarily minted a different facts-side ID: the key correspondence suite failed on the expected ID mismatch and shifted-slot evidence, then returned green after restoration. Final pre-build verification: ride metadata 11/11, server schemas 18/18, client payloads 19/19, focused map/fixture regression 10/10, all 49 unit suites, 90/90 mock e2e, typecheck clean, and lint 0 errors with the same 10 warnings.

## Feature (Transit B, piece 3 of 3): real walking gaps render as neutral dotted runs
Goal: finish Transit B's map rendering by drawing the provider's real walk-step geometry inside transit legs, without inventing connectors, competing with route colours, or changing ordinary whole-leg walk routes.
What should be done: consume walk entries only from authoritative transit `pathSegmentsToNext`; merge consecutive connected paths in provider order within one leg; break on rides, bad geometry, and real gaps; keep dots neutral and lighter than rides; carry changed-leg emphasis without semantic recolouring; and prove paths, styling, separation, ordering, and cleanup through the real map component seam.
What was done (technical): each transit leg now owns a small walk-run accumulator alongside Piece 2's ride renderer. Walk steps decode independently through the already-loaded Google geometry library, retain only paths with at least two finite in-range points and cumulative length greater than 0.5 m, and merge only when `computeDistanceBetween` puts the next boundary within 2 m. Two metres covers one E5 rounding-cell mismatch (about 1.6 m worst-case at the equator) while remaining roughly a pixel at ordinary itinerary zoom; the rounding-equivalent boundary is omitted, and anything farther flushes into a separate native path, so no connector edge is fabricated. Transit entries, missing/throwing/empty/one-point/non-finite/out-of-range/approximately-zero paths, geometry failures, and disconnected boundaries all flush the current run; the accumulator is leg-local and therefore cannot cross legs. Each run is a genuinely dotted `#6B8797` transparent-base polyline using repeated native circle symbols, below the weight/prominence of ride strokes. Changed inbound transit legs add larger, lighter dots underneath while retaining neutral gray; rides keep Piece 2's provider colours and same-colour halos. Walk primaries and underlays use the existing effect-local overlay owner, so rerender, cancellation, unmount, and remount detach them with the ride lines. The harness and Maps stub now record exact decoded paths, nested symbol options, ordering, active ownership, and spherical lengths/distances; focused browser coverage proves all fifteen required merge/break/style/cleanup invariants while ordinary whole-leg WALK stays its single solid ink route. The discriminating revert temporarily disabled boundary merging: the key test failed as expected with 27 active overlays instead of 23, then passed 1/1 after restoration. Verification: focused map suite 9/9, all 48 unit suites, typecheck clean, lint 0 errors (10 existing warnings), webpack production build green, and 90/90 mock e2e including the three current clock-dependent cases. A production server on :3200 returned HTTP 200 and rendered the planning form with no browser warnings/errors; real-provider visual and billing checks remain post-deploy work.

## Feature (Transit B, piece 2 of 3): every ride draws in its own real line colour
Goal: turn Piece 1's display-only step geometry into an honest transit map — one native primary line per usable ride, in the same provider colour as its transit badge — without inventing geometry where the provider supplied none, without pulling Piece 3's walk steps into scope, and without letting the old chartreuse reroute signal overwrite a route's identity.
What should be done: consume `pathSegmentsToNext` inside the existing `ItineraryMap` Maps+geometry loader; decode transit steps independently and in order; keep absent, broken and degenerate ride geometry invisible; use the canonical map ink only when the provider colour is unusable; replace route recolouring with a stable non-colour emphasis for both per-step rides and legacy/plain lines; and make each React effect invocation own and remove its overlays across rerenders, map changes, unmounts and Strict Mode. Prove the behavior through the real component harness, including cleanup, while leaving bounds, marker projection, Piece 1 facts, scheduling and every mutation engine alone.
What was done (technical): **STEP DATA IS AUTHORITATIVE.** A transit leg with `pathSegmentsToNext` now walks those entries once in provider order, skips walk-mode entries entirely, decodes each transit entry with the geometry library already returned by `loadMapLibs`, and creates one solid native primary `google.maps.Polyline` for every path with at least two finite, in-range points. Decode throws, empty strings, empty/one-point results, non-finite/out-of-range points and malformed provider data suppress only that ride; they never trigger the whole-leg polyline or an endpoint chord, so a later valid ride still draws. Six-digit provider hex is retained verbatim and missing/unusable colour falls back to `INK = "#2E6F8A"`. Pre-Piece-1 transit legs can still use a valid whole-leg encoded dashed fallback, but a transit leg with NO provider geometry now draws nothing; the existing solid whole-leg WALK path and its endpoint fallback remain. **ROUTE COLOURS WIN.** The existing destination-stop `changed` flag still identifies the inbound LEG, and that one flag is applied to every valid ride in the leg as a wider, 22%-opacity halo in that ride's own colour underneath an unchanged primary stroke; legacy transit and plain walk use the same-colour halo treatment. No route is recoloured to `#C8F000`, and no timers or animation were added. **OVERLAY OWNERSHIP IS EFFECT-LOCAL.** Each invocation records only the lines it constructs, calls `setMap(null)` for those lines on cleanup/error, refuses to draw after cancellation or onto a replaced map, and cannot let a stale async cleanup detach a newer invocation's overlays; bounds and the HTML marker projection were not touched. The existing mock-only Maps harness gained a selectable four-stop specimen and a rerender control; its Maps stub now decodes deterministic valid/throwing/empty/one-point/non-finite/out-of-range paths and records every overlay's active state/options. Two browser cases prove four ordered primary rides retain red → ink fallback → ink fallback → blue, all four changed rides get matching underlays, bad/missing geometry and the valid whole-leg temptation make no fabricated line, walk steps are never decoded, the ordinary whole-leg walk keeps its solid ink styling, a geometry-free legacy transit leg stays invisible, rerender replaces rather than accumulates overlays, unmount reaches zero active lines, and remount restores the exact count. **DISCRIMINATING REVERT-RUN:** the transit-step render condition was temporarily forced to skip every ride; the key new test went red as expected because it waited for nine active overlays while only the ordinary walk line existed, then the condition was restored and the same test passed. Verification: focused Maps browser suite 6/6; `npm run check` green with lint at 0 errors, typecheck clean, all 48 unit suites, the required webpack production build, and 87/87 mock e2e (including all three current clock-dependent stated-window cases). Scheduler math, swap/reroute/remove logic, prompts, provider routing/requests/parsing, facts validation, marker positioning and Piece 3 walk-step rendering are untouched.

## Plumbing (Transit B, piece 1 of 3): the map gets the leg's REAL shape, step by step — and draws none of it yet
Goal: the map draws one undivided line per leg, because one undivided line is all a leg carries — `encodedPolyline` is the whole journey as a single string, with no seam at the door of the station and no idea which stretch is a walk and which is the 506. Everything the later pieces want to draw (a walk rendered as a walk, a ride in its own line's colour, the shape breaking where you change trains) needs geometry that knows where those seams are. This piece fetches it, parses it, stores it and hands it to the map component. Nothing renders — there is deliberately NO visible change, and the owner does not need to look at the map.
What should be done: widen the Routes field mask by the three step-level fields, read a per-step polyline out of the response, carry it on the leg as a new additive field, validate it in both directions across the wire, and thread it into the three projections that feed `ItineraryMap` — while leaving Build A's rides, board/alight times and transfer markers untouched, and leaving the scheduler literally untouched.
What was done (technical): **THE MASK GREW BY EXACTLY THREE FIELDS** — `routes.legs.steps.polyline.encodedPolyline`, `routes.legs.steps.travelMode`, `routes.legs.steps.staticDuration` — with `routes.legs.steps.transitDetails` and the other three left in place and in order. All three are ESSENTIALS-tier and a request bills at its highest tier (transitDetails is already Pro), so this should not move the SKU: **that is inferred from Google's field-list pricing docs and NOT measured — check one live billing line after deploy.** **THE NEW FIELD IS `pathSegments`,** on `TravelLeg` beside the existing `transitSegments`, shaped `{ mode: "walk" | "transit"; encodedPolyline: string; color?: string | null }[]` in the provider's own step order — walk to the stop, the ride, the transfer walk, the next ride, the walk to the door. `mode` comes from `travelMode` and is never inferred (a `DRIVE` step, or one with no travelMode at all, is skipped rather than guessed); `color` is the ride's own `transitLine.color`, read from the SAME field the strip's badge reads so a line on the map and its bubble cannot disagree, and null on every walk step. **NO GEOMETRY, NO SEGMENT** — a step the provider drew no line for produces nothing, and an empty string is not geometry (stored, it decodes to a line at 0,0). Degenerate steps are kept if they have a line: the parse stays honest and Piece 3 decides what is too short to draw. **`staticDuration` IS REQUESTED AND DELIBERATELY NOT STORED.** It arrives with the geometry and would be the one number here that could be mistaken for a scheduling input; the shelved "the plan under-budgets the platform wait" finding stays shelved, exactly as Build A left the board times. **THE STEPS ARE WALKED ONCE:** `readSteps` is the single traversal and returns both readings — the rides (Build A) and the geometry (this slice) — with `extractTransitSegments` now a one-line accessor over it, so the mock fixture's import and its OUTPUT are unchanged, and `extractPathSegments` is its new sibling. **GEOMETRY ALWAYS COMES FROM THE ROUTE ITS NUMBERS CAME FROM**: a walk-relabeled leg carries the WALK response's steps, never the transit response's, and a test pins it. The field is OMITTED when empty, exactly as `transitSegments` is, which is what keeps every existing `deepStrictEqual` on a leg exact and every stored plan valid. **BOTH VALIDATORS, AND THEY ARE DELIBERATELY ASYMMETRIC.** Server (`parseTravelLegs`) SANITIZES: a malformed segment is dropped and the leg survives, because geometry is a decoration with the whole-leg polyline still behind it and refusing would cost the caller an entire plan over a line on a map; only the field's own shape is refused (not an array, or more than 128 entries). The browser (`isTravelLeg`) REJECTS, like every other guard there — by then the server has already sanitized, so a malformed segment means the payload is not what it claims. Both bound a segment at 4096 chars; the number is written twice because `clientPayloads.ts` runs in the browser and `travel.ts` pulls in server-only modules, the same reason the 1,440-minute and 64-char bounds are already written twice. **THE CAP IS A FILTER AND IT DELETES RATHER THAN ERRORS** — the `max_price` lesson, one file over — so it was probed rather than guessed: on two live cross-town journeys the longest single step was 636 chars, ~6x under it. **THE SCHEDULER WAS NOT TOUCHED**: `schedule.ts` does not appear in the diff at all. `buildSchedule` still attaches the whole leg object and still runs on `totalMinutes`; a live re-check of the same journey returned 86/91 minutes before and after. **THREE PROJECTIONS CARRY IT TO THE MAP** — `stopsFromSchedule`, `stopsFromItinerary` and the `mapHome` memo each gained one line — and `MapStop`/`MapHome` gained `pathSegmentsToNext` (named for its neighbours `polylineToNext`/`legModeToNext`, since `legSegments` on those types already means the RIDES). `ItineraryMap` receives it and does nothing with it: no polyline, no colour, no walk line. Piece 2 is the first visible change. **MOCK: NOTHING ADDED, ON PURPOSE.** `mockLeg`'s `encodedPolyline` is already null, so a fixture that published per-step geometry while publishing no route geometry would describe a provider that does not exist — and any polyline written there is invented geometry that mock e2e would then draw. The parse is covered directly by hand-built provider-shaped responses in `travel.test.ts`; per-step geometry joins real map geometry and transfer markers behind the same live-verify-only wall. **LIVE PROBES (the part no test can reach):** the exact new mask, against the real Routes API, twice — a 9-step single-ride trip and a 21-step two-transfer cross-town trip. Both HTTP 200, every step carried both a polyline and a travelMode, and **`transitDetails` still arrived intact on every ride** (line, board instant, board stop, stop coordinates) — Build A is unaffected, which was the one thing that would have stopped this piece. Then end to end through a production server on :3200 (port 3000 untouched): the real journey came back as 21 segments in order with the three TTC colours on the three rides, was POSTed to the store with a corrupt `{mode:"drive"}` segment spliced in, and read back with 21 segments, the corrupt one gone, the rides/board times/minutes/whole-leg polyline all unchanged. Stored plan: 11 KB with that leg serialized twice, against a 256 KB body cap. **FLAGGED, NOT FIXED, and found by that same round trip:** `parseScheduledStops` does not validate `stops[].travelToNext` at all, so the copy of the leg hanging off the stop kept the corrupt segment while `itinerary.legs` was sanitized — and `rebuildLegs` would copy it back on the next mutation. This is pre-existing and Build A shares it with fields that become MAP MARKERS; it is only reachable by a client posting a hand-crafted body (the browser posts what our own pipeline produced), and the browser's own guard rejects such a plan on read. Closing it means newly refusing stored plans that pass today, which is not this piece's blast radius. **TESTS: +6 travel cases** (order/mode/colour across a five-step journey; a Build A regression pinning the same response's rides, `transit` compat field and 30/35 minutes; no-polyline, unknown-mode, no-mode and empty-string steps all skipped; an over-long line dropped with the leg intact; a relabeled walk drawing the walk route's steps; and a response with no step geometry carrying no key at all), **+4 `schemas` cases** and **+2 `clientPayloads` cases** covering both directions of the wire including the drop-vs-reject split. **REVERT-RUN, discriminating:** disabling the segment push turned exactly the four "geometry is populated" cases red (23/27) while every Build A ride, board-time and coordinate case stayed green — and the "no step geometry → no key" case stayed green too, which is correct, since absence is what the reverted code also produces. Restored, 27/27. **GATE:** `npm run check` green — lint 0 errors with the same 11 long-standing warnings, typecheck clean, 48 unit suites, webpack build (the client bundle builds, so the type-only `PathSegment` import into `ItineraryMap` pulls no server code), **85/85 mock e2e including all three clock-dependent specs**, run at 15:10 local.

## Feature: a stop can be removed, and the day closes the gap behind it
Goal: let someone delete a stop they no longer want, and have the rest of the day re-time itself around the hole — without the removal quietly costing them a venue somewhere else, and without letting them delete their way to a plan that no longer exists.
What should be done: reuse the swap engine's tail cascade rather than writing a second one; splice the stop out and re-settle from its predecessor. Close the gap (later stops slide EARLIER) but clamp each slid stop at its own opening time so it is never scheduled before it opens. Refuse a locked or past stop, refuse the last remaining stop, and leave the itinerary byte-identical on any refusal. Ask inline in the card — arm, then confirm — never a modal, and no undo.
What was done (technical): **THE CASCADE IS `resettleTail`, UNCHANGED, AND THAT IS THE WHOLE DESIGN.** Removing stop N is "re-settle the tail from stop N-1": the cascade's first iteration IS the bridging leg (the predecessor now travels straight to the successor), it prices that leg for real, re-times everything after it, and re-checks each venue. `app/api/itinerary/removeStop.ts` (~420 lines) adds no travel arithmetic, no scheduling rule and no availability rule of its own.

**EXPORT, NOT EXTRACT — and the reason is risk, not taste.** `resettleTail` and its helpers were `swap.ts`-private. Moving the ladder into a shared module would have meant relocating ~600 lines and a web of about eighteen interdependent helpers (`findReplacement`, `buildStop`, `scoped`, `usableForProposal`, `usableThroughSlot`, `occupancyMinutes`, `lastMomentOfSlot`, `proposalLeg`, `planAnchorInbound`, `homeDeparture`, `commitAnchorInbound`, `placeOf`, `weatherFor`, `cloneProposal`, `validLocation`, `clockLabel`, `realDeps`, the `TailChange`/`AnchorInbound` types) out of a 2379-line engine covered by a 2323-line suite — exactly the "restructuring that ripples into swap's behavior" this work was told to avoid. Instead fifteen symbols each gained one `export` keyword: the diff is precisely 15 insertions and 15 deletions, and swap's suites (81 + 13 atomic) stayed green on the first run. The file-size concern the brief raised is answered by `removeStop` living in its own module, which it does. Extraction stays available later as a pure move, now that a second caller exists to justify it.

**THE OPEN-TIME CLAMP IS NEW, AND IT HAD TO BE — `resettleTail` does not clamp, it SUBSTITUTES.** Its reaction to a venue that is not usable at a stop's new arrival is `findReplacement`. That is right for a swap (the user asked for a different venue) and completely wrong for a removal: closing a gap pulls later stops earlier, early enough lands one of them before its own doors open, and the engine would then answer by silently swapping a venue the user never mentioned. Removing your 7pm dinner must never change your 10pm bar. So `resettleTail` gained one optional parameter, `clampEarlierToAvailability`, OFF for every swap path (byte-identical to before) and ON for removal. When a stop would slide earlier than its committed start, `earliestUsableStart` walks forward a minute at a time from the proposed instant to the committed one and takes the first instant that passes — so the stop gives up only as much of the gap as it can legally take, and keeps its venue. It ALWAYS terminates and is always cheap: the ceiling is where the stop already sat, so the worst case is not moving at all, and every step is pure (a one-place `filterPools` plus the availability seam), no provider calls. Deliberately a scan through `deps.isUsableAt` rather than an "opening time" lookup — hours are one answer to "is this usable then", the seam is the answer, and reading hours directly here would create a second definition to drift from the day a reservation API replaces it. Only in the earlier direction: a stop forced LATER (a direct leg longer than the two it replaces) has no earlier instant to protect, and the existing ladder is the honest answer there.

**THE FOUR EDGE CASES.** FIRST stop: no predecessor to anchor on, so the origin is home. The departure instant does NOT change — it is the removed stop's start less the home leg that served it, read off `homeDeparture` BEFORE `commitAnchorInbound` overwrites `homeLeg` — and the new first stop then starts as early as the re-priced real leg allows, floored by `now` and clamped by its own opening time. It can also land LATER, when home is simply further from the second stop than the route through the first was; that is a fact about the map. LAST stop: the predecessor was left holding `travelToNext`, a real leg to a venue no longer in the plan, which `rebuildLegs` would copy into `legs` and the strip would draw as a journey to nothing — `setOutbound(prev, null)` deletes both fields, the same shape `buildSchedule` gives a fresh plan's last stop. (There is NO travel-home leg in this app, contrary to the brief's premise: `schedule.ts` deletes `travelToNext` from the last timed stop. The dangling-predecessor leg was the real hazard.) LOCKED/PAST: refused with the same predicate `swapStop` uses. DOWN TO ZERO: refused, pointing at "End".

**WHY DOWN-TO-ZERO IS LOAD-BEARING, VERIFIED NOT ASSUMED.** An empty `stops` array is not a harmless edge case, it reads as a FINISHED outing: `nextItineraryStatus` asks `stops.every(completed || skipped)` and `[].every(...)` is vacuously TRUE, so the plan reports `status: "completed"`. The by-id GET then acts on it — clears the owner's resume pointer, and for a signed-in account writes the empty plan into history through `archiveConcludedPlan` — and `isResumable` reads the same status, so it can never be opened again. Deleting to zero would file a blank record under someone's name and lose their plan, silently. The guard counts stops that have a VENUE, so a plan left with nothing but weather-skipped slots is refused too (all-skipped trips the same vacuous branch).

**PLAN-THEN-COMMIT, mirroring swap exactly**: everything runs on `cloneProposal`, and the real itinerary is written in one `Object.assign` on the last line of the success path. The route reports a refusal as `changed: false`, so no CAS runs and no version is bumped — confirmed live, where the refused last-stop removal left the plan at version 3 and `status: "planning"`.

**ROUTE: NEW, NOT A MODE OF /swap.** Structurally identical (id shape, rate limit, `updateItinerary` CAS with `expectedVersion`, ETag) but its own file, because swap's body requires a non-empty `refinement` that the engine hands to a model to classify. A removal has nothing to say, and folding it in would mean either inventing a sentinel phrase to put in front of the LLM or branching the route on an absent field. AUTH follows the swap precedent — none beyond the plan id — re-verified against current code: `/swap` and `/reroute` verify no caller, and only `/end` calls `verifyCaller`, because ending a plan writes to a person's history.

**UI: ARM, THEN CONFIRM, IN THE CARD.** A `--danger` token was introduced — the value is not new, this exact red was already written out TEN times across auth errors, the End control, the stop dialog and the history/preferences panels (the investigation said ~4), so naming it makes the armed state the SAME red the app already uses for irreversible rather than an eleventh literal that happens to match. Disarmed it is the quietest control in the card; armed it takes the red across the control AND the card border. "Keep" holds focus so a stray Return never deletes anything; Escape steps back; an outside POINTERDOWN disarms (not click — a click-based dismissal survives under a finger through a scroll, the rule `AccountMenu` already follows). It is a SIBLING of the swap form, never nested: a button inside that form would submit it. No undo, and the copy says so.

**TWO PRE-EXISTING BUGS FOUND AND FIXED — neither is remove-specific.** (1) `page.tsx`'s arrival-time ADAPT path overrode `id`/`name`/`rating`/`priceLevel`/`description` on the replacement but not `currentOpeningHours`, so an adapted stop carried the REPLACED venue's hours forever. Every later availability check reads the stop's own field (`placeOf`), so a plan whose dessert adapted to a late-night bakery still claimed a 9 PM close — and closing a gap in front of it looked like arriving after closing, which swapped the venue out again. The swap engine's tail reflow reads the same field for the same purpose, so this was already reachable without removal. One line. (2) The e2e helpers clicked a stop's card BOX; `.lstrip` is a flex row so every card stretches to the tallest, and adding the Remove control made the selected card taller, pushing other cards' centres below their own select buttons. `swapOn` then typed into a PAGE-WIDE `.lstrip__swapinput`, which resolved to whichever card WAS open — so a mis-aimed click did not fail, it swapped a stop the test never named. Helpers now click `.lstrip__select`, wait for the selection to stick, and scope the input to the card.

**A RACE WORTH RECORDING**: the pipeline auto-selects the FIRST stop as one of its last acts, and that can land after the strip is on screen and `planEvening` has returned. A click on any other card in that gap is silently undone a moment later, and the symptom is not "wrong card selected" — it is the card's inline controls never appearing, which reads exactly like a missing feature. `selectStop` closes it.

**TESTS**: `removeStop.test.ts`, 17 cases — middle/first/last, the clamp and its cascade, down-to-one, down-to-zero (twice, including the all-skipped shape), locked, past, both plan-then-commit empty-diff paths, a genuinely-shut downstream venue still adapting, and the removed venue never being handed back. Plus 3 payload cases and a 4-test e2e spec. NO NEW MOCK SEAM WAS NEEDED, which is itself evidence of the reuse: `removeStop` takes `SwapDeps` built from swap's `realDeps()`, so `mockSwapDeps` already answered everything.

**BOTH REVERT-RUNS DONE, AND THE FIRST ONE FOUND A WEAK TEST.** Disabling the clamp turned the two clamp cases red on the venue-identity assertion — the substitution, exactly as predicted. Disabling the down-to-zero guard turned NOTHING red: `reanchorOnHome` catches the empty case a second time and emits the same words, so a test reading only the message passed with the guard deleted. The guard's real job is refusing BEFORE anything is spent, so both zero cases now also assert the forecast call never happens; re-run, they go red. (Making that assertion work needed a `cityCenter` on the fixture parse — `weatherFor` returns null without one and never calls the seam.)

**GATE**: `npm run check` fully green — lint 0 errors and the same 11 long-standing warnings, typecheck clean, 48 unit suites, build, 85 mock e2e. All 85 passed, including the three CLAUDE.md records as clock-dependent (run at 23:06, late enough that their windows roll to tomorrow). Verified in a real browser on :3200 with :3000 untouched: select → Remove appears → arms red → Confirm removes it; the later stop slid 22:17 → 21:04 and KEPT its venue; a fresh 3-minute bridging leg replaced the old one; the new last stop carries no dangling leg; down to one worked and the last removal was refused with the plan left at version 3 and `status: "planning"`.

FLAGGED, NOT DONE: removing the only UPCOMING stop while a COMPLETED one remains still concludes the plan. That is genuine conclusion — the same thing the clock would do a minute later — not an artifact of the splice, so the zero-guard deliberately does not cover it. Also not done: a removal is never asked about against `plannedEndISO`. A removal normally shortens the day, and the only path that could lengthen it (a longer direct home leg) would need the swap engine's confirm DIALOG, which this slice was scoped away from.

## Migration: the LLM provider moves from Groq to OpenRouter — transport only, same weights
Goal: get off Groq's platform-wide token ceiling — the thing that fails a four-stop plan with a capacity 503 while nothing is actually wrong — without changing anything about what the models are or what they are asked. Same weights, same prompts, same validation ladders: a swap of the pipe, not of the judgment.
What should be done: point the three call sites at OpenRouter's endpoint, read the key from `OPENROUTER_API_KEY`, translate the three model chains to OpenRouter's namespaced spellings of the SAME models, rename the chain-override env vars, and fix the two things that make OpenRouter behave differently from Groq under an otherwise identical request. Change no prompt, no planner/select/swap logic, no parser, and no part of the fallback algorithm except the input its error detection reads.
What was done (technical): **THE BODY IS THE SAME, WHICH IS WHY THIS WAS A TRANSPORT SWAP AT ALL.** OpenRouter takes `messages`, `response_format: {type:"json_object"}` and `temperature: 0` verbatim and returns `choices[0].message.content` the same way, so the three `callGroq` functions became `callModel` with the URL and the key changed and nothing else touched. What did NOT stay the same is where the request is BUILT: the endpoint plus the two fields below now live in ONE file, `app/api/_shared/openrouter.ts` (`OPENROUTER_URL` + `chatCompletionBody`), imported by parse/select/swap. Three literal copies of a field that fails SILENTLY is precisely the shape `models.ts` was created to end, and a fourth call site that forgot one would fail in a way that looks like a model problem rather than a request problem. The URL is HARDCODED — no base-URL env var, by decision. **GOTCHA ONE — `provider: { require_parameters: true }`, and without it JSON mode is a suggestion.** OpenRouter resolves a model id to one of several serving ENDPOINTS, and `response_format` support is a property of the endpoint, not of the model. An endpoint that lacks it does not reject the request: OpenRouter DROPS the parameter and the model answers in prose, which every parser in this pipeline reads as a malformed model — at a rate set by which endpoint routing happened to pick, with no cause anywhere in this repo. The flag pins routing to endpoints supporting every parameter sent, so "no compliant endpoint" surfaces as a 5xx, and a 5xx is exactly what `withModelFallback` advances the chain on. **GOTCHA TWO — a 200 CAN BE A FAILURE.** OpenRouter answers some rejections, a rate limit among them, with HTTP 200 and `{error:{code,message}}` in the BODY. `readProviderJson` only inspected `response.ok`, so that parsed as a normal answer, `choices` came back undefined, the call site threw its own `*_invalid_response` — and because that error carries no `upstreamStatus`, `isModelRetryable` was false and THE CHAIN NEVER ADVANCED. The request burned instead of falling back: the entire failure the chain exists to survive, silently reintroduced by a status code. `readProviderJson` now reads the wrapped code back into `upstreamStatus` after a successful parse. The guard is narrow on BOTH sides on purpose — a body carrying `choices` is never treated as an error, and `error` must be an OBJECT, because Upstash answers a failed command with `{error: "<string>"}` and a fix aimed at one LLM provider must not move Redis. An unreadable code degrades to a retryable 502, since a wrapped error with nothing to read is the provider malfunctioning and asking a different model is the cheap correct answer to that. **THE MODELS ARE THE SAME MODELS.** `meta-llama/llama-3.3-70b-instruct` (planner/select primary), `openai/gpt-oss-120b`, `openai/gpt-oss-20b`, `meta-llama/llama-3.1-8b-instruct` (swap primary) — the gpt-oss ids are identical strings and the llama ids only gained a namespace and a suffix. Nothing was re-picked, and `models.ts` now says so, alongside the honest correction that the per-call-type split can no longer be justified by per-model rate limits (OpenRouter shares account credit) and rests on task fit instead. `GROQ_MODELS_*` → `OPENROUTER_MODELS_*`; the parsing is provider-agnostic and untouched. **THE TIMEOUT IS 45s, up from 20s**, and why it matters is not obvious: `fetchProvider` throws a timeout with NO `failure`, so `isModelRetryable` is false and a timeout does NOT advance the chain — a slow model fails the request outright. Groq's 20s was tuned to an LPU; OpenRouter routes to whichever upstream is serving, and two of three chain entries emit reasoning tokens before answering. Not higher than 45s because two ceilings sit above it (see FLAGGED below). **REASONING IS ASKED FOR ONLY WHERE IT IS MANDATORY, and that is a consequence of gotcha one rather than a preference.** `reasoning: {effort:"low", exclude:true}` goes to the `gpt-oss` entries — less time thinking, and reasoning kept OUT of `message.content`, which is the string `JSON.parse` runs on at all three call sites. It is NOT sent to the llama entries: `require_parameters` filters on this parameter too, and llama-3.3-70b is not a reasoning model, so asking for it would demand endpoints advertising support for something the model does not have and leave the request with no endpoint at all — killing the primary on both the planner and select chains. The gate is therefore the MODEL, not the call site. **`ProviderName` "groq" → "openrouter"**, so error codes and every log line name the provider that actually served the request; `groq_rate_limited` during an OpenRouter incident is the kind of name-that-lies this repo has already paid for twice (`--paper` is blue, `INK` is teal). The test renames follow mechanically, including the test-local `groqCalls`/`groqReplies` identifiers, so `grep -i groq` stays a meaningful question — the only remaining hits are deliberate history in `models.ts`, `openrouter.ts` and `provider.ts`. **NEW COVERAGE FOR BOTH GOTCHAS, because both are new behaviour and neither is reachable from mock e2e** (all 81 short-circuit at `isMockMode()` before any provider call — confirmed at parse, select, and both engines' `realDeps()`): `openrouter.test.ts` (6 cases) pins that every chain model's body carries JSON mode AND require_parameters, that reasoning reaches the gpt-oss entries with `exclude`, that it never reaches the llama entries, and that no chain PRIMARY is reasoning-mandatory; `http.test.ts` gains five cases for the 200-wrapped error, two of which exist to prove it did not overreach (a real completion is never mistaken for one; Upstash's string error is untouched); `modelFallback.test.ts` proves end-to-end through the REAL `readProviderJson` that a body-wrapped 429 reaches the next model; `parse.test.ts` proves the SHIPPED request carries the pair on the correction retry as well as on the first call. Gate: `npm run check` green. **FLAGGED, NOT FIXED — the browser gives up before the server does.** `DEFAULT_CLIENT_FETCH_TIMEOUT_MS` is 15s and no caller overrides it, so a genuinely slow model surfaces as a client timeout regardless of the 45s server budget. That is client plumbing shared by every route and out of scope for a transport swap; it is the first thing to look at if the live probe shows latency. **LIVE-PROBE ITEMS, unprovable offline, in order:** (1) reasoning genuinely lands in `message.reasoning` and never leaks into `content` — a harmony-channel leak would break all three call sites at once and needs a model/param rethink before this ships; (2) a four-stop plan completes with no capacity 503, which is the point of the migration; (3) a category-change swap and a constraint swap still interpret correctly on llama-3.1-8b via OpenRouter; (4) no 504s at 45s on a fat select; (5) a vague "something to do" prompt still produces a full plan on llama-3.3-70b.

### Follow-up (same migration, 2026-08-15): the models were right, the ROUTE to them was slow
Goal: the migration worked and plans were still unusable — a plan took ~21s and died on the browser's own deadline. The diagnosis off OpenRouter's activity breakdown was unambiguous and it was not the chain, the body or the JSON: routing overhead was 59ms and GENERATION was the bottleneck, at 8.9 tokens/sec. OpenRouter had picked a slow serving endpoint, so the fix belongs in the routing policy and nowhere else.
What should be done: ask OpenRouter for the FASTEST endpoint instead of its price-weighted default, bound the cost that opens up, and do both in a way that names no provider and no model so a future chain change inherits it for free. Change nothing else about the request — same model, same messages, same `response_format`, same `temperature`, same reasoning-exclude.
What was done (technical): **`provider.sort: "throughput"` (documented as the `:nitro` equivalent), and it COMPOSES with gotcha one in the only order that is safe.** `require_parameters` narrows the candidate endpoints to those that genuinely honour every parameter sent; `sort` then picks the fastest of what SURVIVES. Fastest COMPLIANT endpoint, never fastest outright — reversed, this would reintroduce the prose replies gotcha one exists to prevent. **THE DEFAULT IS NOT MERELY SLOW, IT IS A LOTTERY, and that reframes the bug.** Price-weighted routing also load-balances, so the same request lands somewhere different each time: measured live against the REAL planner prompt, one call was served by DeepInfra at **5.7 tok/s**, another by AkashML at 43, another by SambaNova at 147. The slow end is not a tail — 5.7 tok/s over a 334-token four-activity plan is a ~58 SECOND generation, past even the 45s server budget, never mind the browser's. That is why the failure looked intermittent. After: **Groq, 288-310 tok/s, ~0.9-1.1s end to end, on both prompts and both runs** — the consistency matters as much as the speed. **DELIBERATELY NOT `order: ["Groq"]`, and this is the design decision worth defending.** A provider pin names something that is not ours: providers serve different subsets of models, so a pin hard-couples the request body to today's chain and breaks SILENTLY the day `models.ts` changes — the exact drift `models.ts` was created to end, re-created one file over. `sort` and `max_price` express "fastest compliant endpoint under a price ceiling" for WHATEVER chain is in force, including an env-overridden one this file has never heard of, so Phase 2 needs no edit here. A test pins the property rather than trusting the comment: the routing block contains no model id and none of `order`/`only`/`ignore`. **`provider.max_price` puts back the bound that throughput-sorting removed** — sorting by speed takes price out of the ordering entirely, so nothing otherwise stops routing at a fast-but-premium endpoint. **THE OBVIOUS NUMBER WAS WRONG, and finding that out was the only real research in this change.** The brief proposed a round $2/$2 as "well above the ~$0.10-0.60 these models cost". That figure is the per-MODEL blend; `max_price` filters per ENDPOINT, so the number that matters is the priciest endpoint, and probing `GET /api/v1/models/{id}/endpoints` for all four chain models found $1.04/M prompt (Together on llama-3.3-70b) and **$2.253/M completion (Cloudflare, same model)**. A `completion: 2` would have silently DELETED a legitimate endpoint — max_price is a filter, so it deletes rather than errors, which is the same class of invisible failure as the two original gotchas. Shipped at `{ prompt: 5, completion: 10 }`: ~4.5x headroom over every real endpoint, still refusing frontier-model pricing. **THE COST WENT UP AND THAT IS THE TRADE.** Groq is genuinely dearer than DeepInfra: ~$0.0020 per planner call against ~$0.0005 on the cheapest endpoint. Roughly 500 planner calls to the dollar, for a ~50x throughput improvement — recorded rather than buried, because "fastest" and "cheapest" were exactly the same question until this change and are not any more. **THE CLIENT DEADLINE, WHICH THE MIGRATION FLAGGED AND DID NOT FIX, IS NOW 25s** (`DEFAULT_CLIENT_FETCH_TIMEOUT_MS`, 15s before). It is a clean single-constant change — no caller overrides it, no test pinned the value (the `15_000`s in `scenarios.spec.ts` are Playwright expect timeouts and unrelated), and all 19 `fetchJson` call sites in `page.tsx` share it. **It is NOT what fixed the latency and the comment says so**: throughput routing did, and 25s is headroom for the tail, where one LLM-backed request can legitimately cost several sequential model calls (a correction retry, then the next model in the chain). Not raised to meet the server's 45s — past ~25s the honest answer to a user watching a spinner is that something is wrong, and every non-LLM route answers in well under a second or is broken. The cost is paid only on failure: a hung request now reports ten seconds later. `provider.ts`'s comment naming the old 15s was corrected in the same pass rather than left to lie. **TESTS: `openrouter.test.ts` 6 → 9 cases** — the composition (sort AND require_parameters, on every chain model), the ceiling (pinned against the REAL probed prices, `prompt >= 1.04` / `completion >= 2.253`, so re-tightening to the round $2 turns it red), and model-agnosticism (no model id, no provider-pinning key). `parse.test.ts`'s shipped-request pin moved from a `deepStrictEqual` literal to the three load-bearing PROPERTIES: a third verbatim copy of that object is the drift this module exists to prevent, and what matters at the route level is that neither the first call nor the correction retry goes out bare. **REVERT-RUNS, discriminating in both directions:** setting the ceiling to the round `{2, 2}` turned exactly the ceiling case red on exactly the completion assertion (7/9) while the throughput and model-agnostic cases stayed green; deleting `sort` turned exactly the speed case red on "must sort by speed" (7/9) while the ceiling case stayed green. Both restored and re-verified 9/9. **GATE: `npm run check` green at 17:07 local** — lint 0 errors with the same 11 long-standing warnings, typecheck clean, 47 unit suites, webpack build, and **81/81 mock e2e including both clock-dependent stated-window cases** (run early enough that `windowUnderway` does not clamp them). **LIVE-VERIFIED against the real API, which is the only place any of this is observable** — mock e2e short-circuits at `isMockMode()` before any provider call, so none of it is reachable there. An A/B harness sent the SHIPPED body (importing the real `chatCompletionBody` and `PLANNER_SYSTEM_PROMPT`, so what was measured is what ships) with and without the new fields: HTTP 200 both ways — which cleared the one risk offline testing genuinely could not, that an unrecognised field 400s and a 400 does NOT advance the chain — JSON parsed cleanly every time, no reasoning field on the llama primary, and the provider moved DeepInfra/AkashML/SambaNova → Groq on every run. Also smoked on a mock production server on :3200 (3000 untouched): a full plan renders end to end through the production build with the new client constant, no console errors. **STILL THE OWNER'S TO EYEBALL, because they need the real Google keys and a real browser session:** a four-stop plan end to end with no capacity 503 (the point of the migration, and the case the old 5.7 tok/s endpoint would have taken ~58s over); a category-change and a constraint swap still classifying correctly on llama-3.1-8b, which is a DIFFERENT chain and was not part of the A/B; and the OpenRouter activity dashboard showing fast providers across a real session rather than the two-call sample measured here.

## Fix: the FIRST leg — the one from your front door — would not open, and it was never the tap
Goal: the home leg renders its collapsed summary and then does nothing when you tap it, while every between-stops transit leg expands correctly. A tap-to-expand display whose first card cannot be tapped is not finished, and the first leg is the one you check before you leave the house.
What should be done: the brief's reading was that the home leg is a separate render branch that got the DISPLAY but not the INTERACTION — so find the seam, wire the home leg into the same handler, the same per-leg selection identity and the same active-OR-selected rule the other legs use, and do not fork. Read the real code first and verify the diagnosis before writing anything.
What was done (technical): **THE DIAGNOSIS WAS WRONG, AND FINDING THAT OUT WAS THE WORK.** There is no home-leg render branch. `ItineraryStrip` renders `<LegCard leg={home.leg} …>` at line 604 and `<LegCard leg={s.legToNext} …>` at line 618 — the SAME component, so the selection state, the `<button class="lstrip__legselect">`, `aria-expanded`, the 44px target and `shouldShowTimeline` are shared by construction and could not be missing from one of them. What differs is one thing: `LegCard` only wraps the summary in a button when `buildTransitTimeline` returns rows, because a control that cannot do anything is worse than no control. **So "not tappable" never meant a missing handler. It meant the timeline came back null, and the honest question was why it did that for the home leg and not the others.** Two separate defects answer it, and both concentrate on the home leg for reasons that are structural rather than coincidental. **DEFECT ONE — `arrive` WAS BOUNDING A PUBLISHED INSTANT, AND IT IS NOT ENTITLED TO.** The staleness rule required leave ≤ board₁ ≤ … ≤ alight_n ≤ arrive. But `arrive` is `leave + totalMinutes`, and `totalMinutes` is the provider's route DURATION plus our margin — and that duration is measured from the moment the traveller starts MOVING, not from the departure instant we asked about. **The wait on the platform is outside it.** That is not a reading of the docs, it is arithmetic off a real response: a leg priced for a 00:00 departure came back `duration: 3025s`, its steps summed to 2616s, and the 409s difference is exactly the transfer waits — the FIRST wait is the one the number leaves out (verified a second way: 3228s of real elapsed journey minus the 204s initial wait is 3024s, the leg duration to the second). So on perfectly fresh data a cross-town leg boarded 15.5 min after we asked and alighted 5.4 min PAST the scheduler's arrival, and the guard threw the whole breakdown away. Measured across real Toronto legs: 2 of 9 suppressed at an 8pm anchor, 0 of 12 at 7pm — it tracks how sparse the service is, so it bites hardest on late hours and long multi-transfer legs, which is the shape the home leg usually has and the shape the breakdown exists for. **THE FIX IS AN ASYMMETRY, STATED OUT LOUD:** three of the four instants are FACTS (leave is the instant this leg was priced at; board and alight are what the agency published) and ARRIVE alone is a forecast, so when the forecast contradicts a published fact the FORECAST yields — the arrive row is dropped and the timeline ends at the last alight, rather than printing an arrival earlier than the ride that delivers you.
